  • Projection of \(\vec a\) on \(\vec b\): scalar \(\dfrac{\vec a\cdot\vec b}{|\vec b|}\), vector \(\dfrac{\vec a\cdot\vec b}{|\vec b|^2}\vec b\). Component of \(\vec a\) perpendicular to \(\vec b\): \(\vec a-\dfrac{\vec a\cdot\vec b}{|\vec b|^2}\vec b\).
  • \(\vec a=(\vec a\cdot\hat i)\hat i+(\vec a\cdot\hat j)\hat j+(\vec a\cdot\hat k)\hat k\).
  • Cauchy–Schwarz: \(|\vec a\cdot\vec b|\le|\vec a||\vec b|\); triangle inequality \(|\vec a+\vec b|\le|\vec a|+|\vec b|\).
  • Internal bisector of the angle between \(\vec a\) and \(\vec b\): along \(\hat a+\hat b\); external: along \(\hat a-\hat b\).
  • Work done by a force \(\vec F\) over displacement \(\vec d\): \(W=\vec F\cdot\vec d\).

Cross product: all results

\[\vec a\times\vec b=|\vec a||\vec b|\sin\theta\,\hat n=\begin{vmatrix}\hat i&\hat j&\hat k\\a_1&a_2&a_3\\b_1&b_2&b_3\end{vmatrix},\qquad|\vec a\times\vec b|^2=|\vec a|^2|\vec b|^2-(\vec a\cdot\vec b)^2\ \text{(Lagrange)}\]
  • Anti-commutative: \(\vec a\times\vec b=-\vec b\times\vec a\); \(\vec a\times\vec a=\vec0\); distributive; \(\hat i\times\hat j=\hat k\), \(\hat j\times\hat k=\hat i\), \(\hat k\times\hat i=\hat j\). Not associative.
  • \(\hat n\) is a unit vector perpendicular to both, with \(\vec a,\vec b,\hat n\) a right-handed system.
  • Area of the parallelogram on \(\vec a,\vec b\): \(|\vec a\times\vec b|\); of the triangle: \(\dfrac12|\vec a\times\vec b|\); of \(\triangle ABC\): \(\dfrac12|\overrightarrow{AB}\times\overrightarrow{AC}|=\dfrac12|\vec a\times\vec b+\vec b\times\vec c+\vec c\times\vec a|\); of a quadrilateral with diagonals \(\vec d_1,\vec d_2\): \(\dfrac12|\vec d_1\times\vec d_2|\).
  • Unit vectors perpendicular to \(\vec a\) and \(\vec b\): \(\pm\dfrac{\vec a\times\vec b}{|\vec a\times\vec b|}\). A vector perpendicular to the plane of \(A,B,C\): \(\overrightarrow{AB}\times\overrightarrow{AC}\).
  • Moment (torque) of \(\vec F\) about \(O\): \(\vec\tau=\vec r\times\vec F\); linear velocity \(\vec v=\vec\omega\times\vec r\).

Scalar triple product

\[[\vec a\ \vec b\ \vec c]=\vec a\cdot(\vec b\times\vec c)=\begin{vmatrix}a_1&a_2&a_3\\b_1&b_2&b_3\\c_1&c_2&c_3\end{vmatrix}\]
  • Cyclic: \([\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]=[\vec b\,\vec c\,\vec a]=[\vec c\,\vec a\,\vec b]\); swapping two vectors changes the sign; \(\vec a\cdot(\vec b\times\vec c)=(\vec a\times\vec b)\cdot\vec c\). Linear in each vector.
  • Zero iff coplanar (or two vectors are parallel or one is zero).
  • Volume of the parallelepiped \(=|[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]|\); of the tetrahedron \(=\dfrac16|[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]|\); of the triangular prism \(=\dfrac12|[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]|\).
  • \([\vec a+\vec b\ \ \vec b+\vec c\ \ \vec c+\vec a]=2[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]\); \([\vec a\times\vec b\ \ \vec b\times\vec c\ \ \vec c\times\vec a]=[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]^2\); \([\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]^2=\begin{vmatrix}\vec a\cdot\vec a&\vec a\cdot\vec b&\vec a\cdot\vec c\\\vec b\cdot\vec a&\vec b\cdot\vec b&\vec b\cdot\vec c\\\vec c\cdot\vec a&\vec c\cdot\vec b&\vec c\cdot\vec c\end{vmatrix}\).
  • \([\hat i\,\hat j\,\hat k]=1\). For unit vectors the maximum of \([\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]\) is 1 (mutually perpendicular).

Vector triple product and product identities

\[\vec a\times(\vec b\times\vec c)=(\vec a\cdot\vec c)\vec b-(\vec a\cdot\vec b)\vec c,\qquad(\vec a\times\vec b)\times\vec c=(\vec a\cdot\vec c)\vec b-(\vec b\cdot\vec c)\vec a\]\[(\vec a\times\vec b)\cdot(\vec c\times\vec d)=(\vec a\cdot\vec c)(\vec b\cdot\vec d)-(\vec a\cdot\vec d)(\vec b\cdot\vec c)\]\[(\vec a\times\vec b)\times(\vec c\times\vec d)=[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec d]\vec c-[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]\vec d=[\vec a\,\vec c\,\vec d]\vec b-[\vec b\,\vec c\,\vec d]\vec a\]\[\vec a\times(\vec b\times\vec c)+\vec b\times(\vec c\times\vec a)+\vec c\times(\vec a\times\vec b)=\vec0\quad\text{(Jacobi)}\]
  • \(\vec a\times(\vec b\times\vec c)\) lies in the plane of \(\vec b,\vec c\); \((\vec a\times\vec b)\times\vec c\) lies in the plane of \(\vec a,\vec b\).
  • Reciprocal system: \(\vec a'=\dfrac{\vec b\times\vec c}{[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]}\), \(\vec b'=\dfrac{\vec c\times\vec a}{[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]}\), \(\vec c'=\dfrac{\vec a\times\vec b}{[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]}\); then \(\vec a\cdot\vec a'=1\), \(\vec a\cdot\vec b'=0\), and \([\vec a'\,\vec b'\,\vec c']=\dfrac1{[\vec a\,\vec b\,\vec c]}\).

Solving vector equations

  • \(\vec a\cdot\vec x=c\) (with \(\vec a\ne\vec 0\)): \(\vec x=\dfrac{c}{|\vec a|^2}\vec a+\vec y\) with \(\vec y\perp\vec a\).
  • \(\vec a\times\vec x=\vec b\) (requires \(\vec a\cdot\vec b=0\)): \(\vec x=\dfrac{\vec b\times\vec a}{|\vec a|^2}+\lambda\vec a\).
  • \(\vec a\cdot\vec x=c\) and \(\vec a\times\vec x=\vec b\) together: \(\vec x=\dfrac{c\,\vec a+\vec b\times\vec a}{|\vec a|^2}\).
  • If \(\vec x\cdot\vec a=\vec x\cdot\vec b=\vec x\cdot\vec c=0\) for non-coplanar \(\vec a,\vec b,\vec c\), then \(\vec x=\vec 0\). If \(\vec x\cdot\vec a=0\) and \(\vec x\cdot\vec b=0\) then \(\vec x\parallel\vec a\times\vec b\).
  • Reflection of \(\vec r\) in a plane with unit normal \(\hat n\): \(\vec r-2(\vec r\cdot\hat n)\hat n\). Rotation of \(\vec v\) through \(\theta\) about the unit axis \(\hat k\) (Rodrigues): \(\vec v\cos\theta+(\hat k\times\vec v)\sin\theta+\hat k(\hat k\cdot\vec v)(1-\cos\theta)\).
  • Vector form of a plane through \(A\) with normal \(\vec n\): \((\vec r-\vec a)\cdot\vec n=0\); of a sphere with centre \(\vec c\): \(|\vec r-\vec c|=R\); with diameter ends \(\vec a,\vec b\): \((\vec r-\vec a)\cdot(\vec r-\vec b)=0\).

Geometry by vectors

  • Medians meet at the centroid in the ratio \(2:1\). Angle in a semicircle is \(90^\circ\): \((\vec r-\vec a)\cdot(\vec r+\vec a)=|\vec r|^2-|\vec a|^2=0\).
  • Cosine rule: \(|\vec b-\vec c|^2=|\vec b|^2+|\vec c|^2-2\vec b\cdot\vec c\). Apollonius: \(AB^2+AC^2=2(AD^2+BD^2)\) for the median \(AD\).
  • Diagonals of a rhombus are perpendicular; of a rectangle equal. For \(ABCD\) a parallelogram \(\vec a+\vec c=\vec b+\vec d\).
  • The three altitudes of a triangle are concurrent (orthocentre); the three perpendicular bisectors are concurrent (circumcentre).
  • Distance of the point \(\vec p\) from the line \(\vec r=\vec a+\lambda\vec b\): \(\dfrac{|(\vec p-\vec a)\times\vec b|}{|\vec b|}\); foot of the perpendicular: \(\vec a+\dfrac{(\vec p-\vec a)\cdot\vec b}{|\vec b|^2}\vec b\).
  • Shortest distance between skew lines \(\vec r=\vec a_1+\lambda\vec b_1\), \(\vec r=\vec a_2+\mu\vec b_2\): \(\dfrac{|(\vec a_2-\vec a_1)\cdot(\vec b_1\times\vec b_2)|}{|\vec b_1\times\vec b_2|}\).
27
Vectors & 3D

3-Dimensional Geometry

Vector representation of a point

Position vector of \(P(x,y,z)\):

\[x\hat i+y\hat j+z\hat k\]

Distance formula

\[AB=\sqrt{(x_1-x_2)^2+(y_1-y_2)^2+(z_1-z_2)^2}=|\overrightarrow{OB}-\overrightarrow{OA}|\]

Distance of \(P\) from the axes

\[PA=\sqrt{y^2+z^2},\ PB=\sqrt{z^2+x^2},\ PC=\sqrt{x^2+y^2}\]

Section formula

\[x=\frac{mx_2+nx_1}{m+n},\quad y=\frac{my_2+ny_1}{m+n},\quad z=\frac{mz_2+nz_1}{m+n}\]

Mid point: \(x=\dfrac{x_1+x_2}2,\ y=\dfrac{y_1+y_2}2,\ z=\dfrac{z_1+z_2}2\)

Direction cosines and direction ratios

  • If \(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\) are the angles a directed line makes with the positive x, y and z axes, then \(\cos\alpha,\cos\beta,\cos\gamma\) are its direction cosines, denoted \(\ell,m,n\).
  • \(\ell^2+m^2+n^2=1\)
  • If \(a,b,c\) are proportional to \(\ell,m,n\), they are direction ratios.
\[\ell=\pm\frac a{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}},\quad m=\pm\frac b{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}},\quad n=\pm\frac c{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}}\]

For \(P(x_1,y_1,z_1)\) and \(Q(x_2,y_2,z_2)\), the direction ratios of \(PQ\) are \(a=x_2-x_1,\ b=y_2-y_1,\ c=z_2-z_1\), and the direction cosines are

\[\ell=\frac{x_2-x_1}{|PQ|},\quad m=\frac{y_2-y_1}{|PQ|},\quad n=\frac{z_2-z_1}{|PQ|}\]

Angle between two line segments

\[\cos\theta=\left|\frac{a_1a_2+b_1b_2+c_1c_2}{\sqrt{a_1^2+b_1^2+c_1^2}\,\sqrt{a_2^2+b_2^2+c_2^2}}\right|\]

Perpendicular if \(a_1a_2+b_1b_2+c_1c_2=0\). Parallel if \(\dfrac{a_1}{a_2}=\dfrac{b_1}{b_2}=\dfrac{c_1}{c_2}\).

Projection of a segment on a line

Projection of \(PQ\) on a line with direction cosines \(\ell,m,n\):

\[|\ell(x_2-x_1)+m(y_2-y_1)+n(z_2-z_1)|\]

Equation of a plane

General form: \(ax+by+cz+d=0\), where \(a,b,c\) are not all zero and \(a,b,c,d\in R\).

  • Normal form: \(\ell x+my+nz=p\)
  • Plane through \((x_1,y_1,z_1)\): \(a(x-x_1)+b(y-y_1)+c(z-z_1)=0\)
  • Intercept form: \(\dfrac xa+\dfrac yb+\dfrac zc=1\)
  • Vector form: \((\vec r-\vec a)\cdot\vec n=0\) or \(\vec r\cdot\vec n=\vec a\cdot\vec n\)
  • Parallel plane: any plane parallel to \(ax+by+cz+d=0\) is \(ax+by+cz+\lambda=0\). Distance between \(ax+by+cz+d_1=0\) and \(ax+by+cz+d_2=0\) is \(\dfrac{|d_1-d_2|}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}}\)
  • Through a given point, parallel to two vectors: \(\vec r=\vec a+\lambda\vec b+\mu\vec c\) (parametric, \(\lambda,\mu\) scalars), or \(\vec r\cdot(\vec b\times\vec c)=\vec a\cdot(\vec b\times\vec c)\) (non-parametric)

A plane and a point

Distance of \((x',y',z')\) from the plane \(ax+by+cz+d=0\):

\[\frac{ax'+by'+cz'+d}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}}\]

Perpendicular distance from a point \((\vec a)\) to the plane \(\vec r\cdot\vec n=d\):

\[p=\frac{|\vec a\cdot\vec n-d|}{|\vec n|}\]

Foot \((x',y',z')\) of the perpendicular from \((x_1,y_1,z_1)\) to the plane:

\[\frac{x'-x_1}a=\frac{y'-y_1}b=\frac{z'-z_1}c=-\frac{ax_1+by_1+cz_1+d}{a^2+b^2+c^2}\]

Image of \(P(x_1,y_1,z_1)\) in the plane:

\[\frac{x'-x_1}a=\frac{y'-y_1}b=\frac{z'-z_1}c=-2\,\frac{ax_1+by_1+cz_1+d}{a^2+b^2+c^2}\]

Angle between two planes

\[\cos\theta=\left|\frac{aa'+bb'+cc'}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}\,\sqrt{a'^2+b'^2+c'^2}}\right|\]

Perpendicular if \(aa'+bb'+cc'=0\); parallel if \(\dfrac a{a'}=\dfrac b{b'}=\dfrac c{c'}\).

Vector form: for planes \(\vec r\cdot\vec n_1=d_1\) and \(\vec r\cdot\vec n_2=d_2\):

\[\cos\theta=\frac{\vec n_1\cdot\vec n_2}{|\vec n_1||\vec n_2|}\]

Perpendicular if \(\vec n_1\cdot\vec n_2=0\); parallel if \(\vec n_1=\lambda\vec n_2\).

Angle bisectors

The planes bisecting the angle between \(a_1x+b_1y+c_1z+d_1=0\) and \(a_2x+b_2y+c_2z+d_2=0\):

\[\frac{a_1x+b_1y+c_1z+d_1}{\sqrt{a_1^2+b_1^2+c_1^2}}=\pm\frac{a_2x+b_2y+c_2z+d_2}{\sqrt{a_2^2+b_2^2+c_2^2}}\]

Acute or obtuse bisector: first make both constant terms positive. Then \(a_1a_2+b_1b_2+c_1c_2\gt0\) means the origin lies in the obtuse angle, and \(\lt0\) means the origin lies in the acute angle.

Family of planes

Any plane through the intersection of \(a_1x+b_1y+c_1z+d_1=0\) and \(a_2x+b_2y+c_2z+d_2=0\):

\[a_1x+b_1y+c_1z+d_1+\lambda(a_2x+b_2y+c_2z+d_2)=0\]

For planes \(\vec r\cdot\vec n_1=d_1\) and \(\vec r\cdot\vec n_2=d_2\): \(\ \vec r\cdot(\vec n_1+\lambda\vec n_2)=d_1+\lambda d_2\), where \(\lambda\) is an arbitrary scalar.

Volume of a tetrahedron

Vertices \(A(x_1,y_1,z_1),B(x_2,y_2,z_2),C(x_3,y_3,z_3),D(x_4,y_4,z_4)\):

\[V=\frac16\begin{vmatrix}x_1&y_1&z_1&1\\x_2&y_2&z_2&1\\x_3&y_3&z_3&1\\x_4&y_4&z_4&1\end{vmatrix}\]

Equation of a line

  • A straight line is the intersection of two planes: \(a_1x+b_1y+c_1z+d_1=0\) and \(a_2x+b_2y+c_2z+d_2=0\).
  • Symmetric form: \(\dfrac{x-x_1}a=\dfrac{y-y_1}b=\dfrac{z-z_1}c=r\)
  • Vector equation: \(\vec r=\vec a+\lambda\vec b\)
\[\frac{x-x_1}a=\frac{y-y_1}b=\frac{z-z_1}c\iff\vec r=(x_1\hat i+y_1\hat j+z_1\hat k)+\lambda(a\hat i+b\hat j+c\hat k)\]

Angle between a plane and a line

For the line \(\dfrac{x-x_1}\ell=\dfrac{y-y_1}m=\dfrac{z-z_1}n\) and the plane \(ax+by+cz+d=0\):

\[\sin\theta=\left|\frac{a\ell+bm+cn}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}\,\sqrt{\ell^2+m^2+n^2}}\right|\]

Vector form: for the line \(\vec r=\vec a+\lambda\vec b\) and plane \(\vec r\cdot\vec n=d\):

\[\sin\theta=\left|\frac{\vec b\cdot\vec n}{|\vec b||\vec n|}\right|\]

Perpendicular: \(\dfrac\ell a=\dfrac mb=\dfrac nc\), or \(\vec b\times\vec n=\vec0\)

Parallel: \(a\ell+bm+cn=0\), or \(\vec b\cdot\vec n=0\)

Condition for a line to lie in a plane

Cartesian: the line \(\dfrac{x-x_1}\ell=\dfrac{y-y_1}m=\dfrac{z-z_1}n\) lies in the plane \(ax+by+cz+d=0\) if \(ax_1+by_1+cz_1+d=0\) and \(a\ell+bm+cn=0\).

Vector: the line \(\vec r=\vec a+\lambda\vec b\) lies in the plane \(\vec r\cdot\vec n=d\) if \(\vec b\cdot\vec n=0\) and \(\vec a\cdot\vec n=d\).

Skew lines

Lines that are neither parallel nor coplanar (non-intersecting) are skew lines. For

\[\frac{x-\alpha}\ell=\frac{y-\beta}m=\frac{z-\gamma}n\quad\&\quad\frac{x-\alpha'}{\ell'}=\frac{y-\beta'}{m'}=\frac{z-\gamma'}{n'}\]\[\Delta=\begin{vmatrix}\alpha'-\alpha&\beta'-\beta&\gamma'-\gamma\\\ell&m&n\\\ell'&m'&n'\end{vmatrix}\ne0\ \Rightarrow\ \text{the lines are skew}\]

Shortest distance between \(\vec r=\vec a_1+\lambda\vec b_1\) and \(\vec r=\vec a_2+\lambda\vec b_2\):

\[d=\left|\frac{(\vec a_2-\vec a_1)\cdot(\vec b_1\times\vec b_2)}{|\vec b_1\times\vec b_2|}\right|\]

Skew condition (vector form): \((\vec b_1\times\vec b_2)\cdot(\vec a_2-\vec a_1)\ne0\)

Shortest distance between parallel lines \(\vec r=\vec a_1+\lambda\vec b\) and \(\vec r=\vec a_2+\mu\vec b\):

\[d=\left|\frac{(\vec a_2-\vec a_1)\times\vec b}{|\vec b|}\right|\]

Coplanarity of two lines \(\vec r=\vec a+\lambda\vec b\) and \(\vec r=\vec c+\mu\vec d\):

\[[\vec a-\vec c\ \ \vec b\ \ \vec d]=0\]

Sphere

General equation:

\[x^2+y^2+z^2+2ux+2vy+2wz+d=0\]

Centre \((-u,-v,-w)\) and radius \(\sqrt{u^2+v^2+w^2-d}\).

Direction cosines and ratios: relations

  • A line making angles \(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\) with the axes has direction cosines \(l=\cos\alpha,m=\cos\beta,n=\cos\gamma\); \(l^2+m^2+n^2=1\), so \(\sin^2\alpha+\sin^2\beta+\sin^2\gamma=2\) and \(\cos2\alpha+\cos2\beta+\cos2\gamma=-1\).
  • Direction ratios \(a,b,c\) (any proportional triple): \(l,m,n=\dfrac{\pm(a,b,c)}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}}\). For the line through \(P(x_1,y_1,z_1)\), \(Q(x_2,y_2,z_2)\): ratios \(x_2-x_1,\ y_2-y_1,\ z_2-z_1\).
  • The coordinate axes have direction cosines \((1,0,0),(0,1,0),(0,0,1)\); a line equally inclined to the axes has \(l=m=n=\pm\dfrac1{\sqrt3}\) (angle \(\cos^{-1}\frac1{\sqrt3}\approx54.74^\circ\)).
  • Angle between lines with ratios \((a_1,b_1,c_1)\), \((a_2,b_2,c_2)\): \(\cos\theta=\dfrac{|a_1a_2+b_1b_2+c_1c_2|}{\sqrt{a_1^2+b_1^2+c_1^2}\sqrt{a_2^2+b_2^2+c_2^2}}\). Parallel iff ratios proportional; perpendicular iff \(a_1a_2+b_1b_2+c_1c_2=0\).
  • A line perpendicular to two lines with ratios \((a_1,b_1,c_1)\), \((a_2,b_2,c_2)\) has ratios \((b_1c_2-b_2c_1,\ c_1a_2-c_2a_1,\ a_1b_2-a_2b_1)\) (cross product).
  • Projection of the segment \(PQ\) on a line with cosines \(l,m,n\): \(|(x_2-x_1)l+(y_2-y_1)m+(z_2-z_1)n|\).

Planes: all standard forms

FormEquation
General\(ax+by+cz+d=0\); normal vector \((a,b,c)\)
Normal form\(lx+my+nz=p\) (\(p\gt0\) is the distance from the origin, \(l,m,n\) the direction cosines of the normal)
Intercept form\(\dfrac xa+\dfrac yb+\dfrac zc=1\)
Through \((x_1,y_1,z_1)\) with normal \((a,b,c)\)\(a(x-x_1)+b(y-y_1)+c(z-z_1)=0\)
Through three points\(\begin{vmatrix}x-x_1&y-y_1&z-z_1\\x_2-x_1&y_2-y_1&z_2-z_1\\x_3-x_1&y_3-y_1&z_3-z_1\end{vmatrix}=0\)
Through a point, parallel to two directions\(\begin{vmatrix}x-x_1&y-y_1&z-z_1\\l_1&m_1&n_1\\l_2&m_2&n_2\end{vmatrix}=0\)
Vector forms\(\vec r\cdot\vec n=d\); \(\vec r=\vec a+\lambda\vec b+\mu\vec c\); \([\vec r-\vec a,\ \vec b,\ \vec c]=0\)
Coordinate planes\(x=0\), \(y=0\), \(z=0\); the plane \(x=k\) is parallel to the \(yz\)-plane
Parallel to an axis\(ax+by+d=0\parallel z\)-axis (no \(z\) term); through an axis: \(ax+by=0\) contains the \(z\)-axis; through the origin: \(d=0\)

Planes: distances, feet, images and sides

  • Distance of \((x_1,y_1,z_1)\) from \(ax+by+cz+d=0\): \(\dfrac{|ax_1+by_1+cz_1+d|}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}}\). Distance between parallel planes with constants \(d_1,d_2\): \(\dfrac{|d_1-d_2|}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}}\).
  • Foot \(F\) and image \(I\) of \(P(x_1,y_1,z_1)\):
\[\frac{x-x_1}a=\frac{y-y_1}b=\frac{z-z_1}c=-\frac{ax_1+by_1+cz_1+d}{a^2+b^2+c^2}\ (\text{foot}),\quad\text{same with factor }-\frac{2(\cdots)}{a^2+b^2+c^2}\ (\text{image})\]
  • Two points are on the same side of the plane iff \(ax+by+cz+d\) has the same sign at both; the origin and \(P\) are on the same side iff \(d\) and \(ax_1+by_1+cz_1+d\) have the same sign.
  • The plane meeting the axes at \(A(a,0,0)\), \(B(0,b,0)\), \(C(0,0,c)\): centroid of \(\triangle ABC\) is \(\left(\dfrac a3,\dfrac b3,\dfrac c3\right)\); volume of \(OABC\) is \(\dfrac{|abc|}6\); area of \(\triangle ABC=\dfrac12\sqrt{a^2b^2+b^2c^2+c^2a^2}\).
  • Angle between planes \(a_1x+b_1y+c_1z+d_1=0\) and \(a_2x+b_2y+c_2z+d_2=0\): \(\cos\theta=\dfrac{|a_1a_2+b_1b_2+c_1c_2|}{\sqrt{\sum a_1^2}\sqrt{\sum a_2^2}}\); parallel iff coefficients proportional; perpendicular iff \(a_1a_2+b_1b_2+c_1c_2=0\).

Straight line in space

  • Symmetric form: \(\dfrac{x-x_1}l=\dfrac{y-y_1}m=\dfrac{z-z_1}n=\lambda\); point \((x_1+\lambda l,\ y_1+\lambda m,\ z_1+\lambda n)\). Two-point form: \(\dfrac{x-x_1}{x_2-x_1}=\dfrac{y-y_1}{y_2-y_1}=\dfrac{z-z_1}{z_2-z_1}\). Vector form \(\vec r=\vec a+\lambda\vec b\); or \((\vec r-\vec a)\times\vec b=\vec0\).
  • As intersection of two planes: \(a_1x+b_1y+c_1z+d_1=0=a_2x+b_2y+c_2z+d_2\); direction \(=\vec n_1\times\vec n_2\), i.e. ratios \((b_1c_2-b_2c_1,\ c_1a_2-c_2a_1,\ a_1b_2-a_2b_1)\). To convert, put one coordinate (say \(z=0\)) to get a point.
  • Planes through this line: \(P_1+\lambda P_2=0\). The plane through the line and the point \(Q\): fix \(\lambda\) by substituting \(Q\).
  • Angle between line (ratios \(l,m,n\)) and plane (normal \(a,b,c\)): \(\sin\phi=\dfrac{|al+bm+cn|}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}\sqrt{l^2+m^2+n^2}}\). Line parallel to plane iff \(al+bm+cn=0\); perpendicular iff \(\dfrac al=\dfrac bm=\dfrac cn\).
  • Line lies in the plane iff \(al+bm+cn=0\) and the point \((x_1,y_1,z_1)\) satisfies the plane equation. Line meets the plane at the point whose parameter satisfies \(a(x_1+\lambda l)+b(y_1+\lambda m)+c(z_1+\lambda n)+d=0\).

Point and line: distance, foot, image; two lines

  • Distance of \(P\) from the line through \(A\) with direction \(\vec d\): \(\dfrac{|\overrightarrow{AP}\times\vec d|}{|\vec d|}\).
  • Foot of the perpendicular from \(P(p_1,p_2,p_3)\) to \(\dfrac{x-x_1}l=\dfrac{y-y_1}m=\dfrac{z-z_1}n\): the point with \(\lambda=\dfrac{l(p_1-x_1)+m(p_2-y_1)+n(p_3-z_1)}{l^2+m^2+n^2}\); the image is obtained by doubling the displacement from \(P\) to the foot.
  • Equation of the perpendicular from \(P\) to the line: join \(P\) to the foot.
  • Lines \(L_1:\ \dfrac{x-x_1}{l_1}=\dfrac{y-y_1}{m_1}=\dfrac{z-z_1}{n_1}\) and \(L_2:\ \dfrac{x-x_2}{l_2}=\dfrac{y-y_2}{m_2}=\dfrac{z-z_2}{n_2}\) intersect (are coplanar) iff
\[\begin{vmatrix}x_2-x_1&y_2-y_1&z_2-z_1\\l_1&m_1&n_1\\l_2&m_2&n_2\end{vmatrix}=0\]
  • Plane containing two intersecting (or parallel) lines: normal \(=\vec d_1\times\vec d_2\) (use \(\vec d_1\times\overrightarrow{P_1P_2}\) if parallel). Distance between parallel lines: \(\dfrac{|\overrightarrow{P_1P_2}\times\vec d|}{|\vec d|}\).

Skew lines and the shortest distance

\[d=\frac{\left|(\vec a_2-\vec a_1)\cdot(\vec b_1\times\vec b_2)\right|}{|\vec b_1\times\vec b_2|}=\frac{\left|\begin{vmatrix}x_2-x_1&y_2-y_1&z_2-z_1\\l_1&m_1&n_1\\l_2&m_2&n_2\end{vmatrix}\right|}{\sqrt{(m_1n_2-m_2n_1)^2+(n_1l_2-n_2l_1)^2+(l_1m_2-l_2m_1)^2}}\]
  • The common perpendicular has direction \(\vec b_1\times\vec b_2=(A,B,C)\) and is the intersection of the planes \(\begin{vmatrix}x-x_1&y-y_1&z-z_1\\l_1&m_1&n_1\\A&B&C\end{vmatrix}=0\) and \(\begin{vmatrix}x-x_2&y-y_2&z-z_2\\l_2&m_2&n_2\\A&B&C\end{vmatrix}=0\).
  • Lines are skew iff they are neither parallel nor intersecting (\(d\ne0\)); if \(d=0\) and not parallel they meet.
  • The feet of the common perpendicular: put general points \(P_1+\lambda\vec b_1\), \(P_2+\mu\vec b_2\) and impose \(\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\vec b_1=0=\overrightarrow{PQ}\cdot\vec b_2\).
  • In a cube of edge \(a\): shortest distance between a body diagonal and a skew edge \(=\dfrac a{\sqrt2}\); between a body diagonal and a skew face diagonal \(=\dfrac a{\sqrt6}\).

Sphere: equations and tangent plane

FormEquation
Centre \((a,b,c)\), radius \(r\)\((x-a)^2+(y-b)^2+(z-c)^2=r^2\)
General\(x^2+y^2+z^2+2ux+2vy+2wz+d=0\); centre \((-u,-v,-w)\), \(r=\sqrt{u^2+v^2+w^2-d}\)
Diameter form\((x-x_1)(x-x_2)+(y-y_1)(y-y_2)+(z-z_1)(z-z_2)=0\)
Through four pointsthe \(5\times5\) determinant with rows \((x^2+y^2+z^2,\ x,\ y,\ z,\ 1)\) for the variable point and for each of the four given points, set equal to \(0\)
Through origin / touches coordinate planes\(d=0\) / \((x\mp r)^2+(y\mp r)^2+(z\mp r)^2=r^2\)
Intercept on the \(x\)-axis\(2\sqrt{u^2-d}\) (similarly \(y,z\))
Tangent plane at \((x_1,y_1,z_1)\)\(xx_1+yy_1+zz_1+u(x+x_1)+v(y+y_1)+w(z+z_1)+d=0\)
Tangent plane for \(x^2+y^2+z^2=r^2\)\(xx_1+yy_1+zz_1=r^2\)
  • Plane \(ax+by+cz+d'=0\) touches the sphere iff its distance from the centre equals \(r\). Normal to the sphere at \(P\) passes through the centre. Length of the tangent from \((x_1,y_1,z_1)\): \(\sqrt{S_1}\). Volume \(\dfrac43\pi r^3\), surface area \(4\pi r^2\).

Sphere: sections, systems and angle

  • The section by a plane at distance \(p\) from the centre is a circle of radius \(\sqrt{r^2-p^2}\), with centre at the foot of the perpendicular from the sphere’s centre; it is a great circle when \(p=0\).
  • A circle in space: a sphere together with a plane. Spheres through the circle \(S=0,\ P=0\): \(S+\lambda P=0\).
  • Spheres through the intersection of two spheres: \(S_1+\lambda S_2=0\); the plane \(S_1-S_2=0\) is the radical plane.
  • Angle between two spheres: \(\cos\theta=\dfrac{r_1^2+r_2^2-d^2}{2r_1r_2}=\dfrac{2(u_1u_2+v_1v_2+w_1w_2)-d_1-d_2}{2\sqrt{u_1^2+v_1^2+w_1^2-d_1}\sqrt{u_2^2+v_2^2+w_2^2-d_2}}\); orthogonal iff \(2(u_1u_2+v_1v_2+w_1w_2)=d_1+d_2\).
  • Spheres touch externally iff the distance between their centres is \(r_1+r_2\), internally iff it is \(|r_1-r_2|\).
  • Polar plane of a point \(P\) wrt the sphere is the plane \(T=0\); a tangent line from an external point forms the tangent cone with vertex \(P\).

Standard solids: cube, cuboid, tetrahedron

  • Cuboid \(a\times b\times c\): diagonal \(\sqrt{a^2+b^2+c^2}\); the four body diagonals of a cube meet at the centre; angle between two body diagonals \(\cos^{-1}\dfrac13\); between a body diagonal and an edge \(\cos^{-1}\dfrac1{\sqrt3}\).
  • Regular tetrahedron of edge \(a\): height \(a\sqrt{\tfrac23}\); volume \(\dfrac{a^3}{6\sqrt2}\); circumradius \(\dfrac{a\sqrt6}4\); inradius \(\dfrac{a\sqrt6}{12}\); dihedral angle \(\cos^{-1}\dfrac13\approx70.53^\circ\); opposite edges are perpendicular.
  • Tetrahedron with vertices \(A,B,C,D\): volume \(\dfrac16\left|\det\begin{pmatrix}x_1-x_4&y_1-y_4&z_1-z_4\\x_2-x_4&y_2-y_4&z_2-z_4\\x_3-x_4&y_3-y_4&z_3-z_4\end{pmatrix}\right|\); centroid \(\dfrac14\sum\) of vertices.
  • Projection of a plane figure of area \(A\) onto a plane making dihedral angle \(\theta\): \(A\cos\theta\). Projection of a segment onto a plane: \(|\overrightarrow{AB}-(\overrightarrow{AB}\cdot\hat n)\hat n|\).
  • Reflection of a line in a plane: reflect two of its points (use the image formula) and join.
28
Trigonometry

Solution of Triangle

Sine rule

\[\frac a{\sin A}=\frac b{\sin B}=\frac c{\sin C}\]

Cosine formula

\[\cos A=\frac{b^2+c^2-a^2}{2bc}\qquad\cos B=\frac{c^2+a^2-b^2}{2ca}\qquad\cos C=\frac{a^2+b^2-c^2}{2ab}\]

Projection formula

\[a=b\cos C+c\cos B\qquad b=c\cos A+a\cos C\qquad c=a\cos B+b\cos A\]

Napier's analogy (tangent rule)

\[\tan\frac{B-C}2=\frac{b-c}{b+c}\cot\frac A2\qquad\tan\frac{C-A}2=\frac{c-a}{c+a}\cot\frac B2\qquad\tan\frac{A-B}2=\frac{a-b}{a+b}\cot\frac C2\]

Half-angle functions

Here \(s=\dfrac{a+b+c}2\) is the semi-perimeter.

\[\sin\frac A2=\sqrt{\frac{(s-b)(s-c)}{bc}}\quad\sin\frac B2=\sqrt{\frac{(s-c)(s-a)}{ca}}\quad\sin\frac C2=\sqrt{\frac{(s-a)(s-b)}{ab}}\]\[\cos\frac A2=\sqrt{\frac{s(s-a)}{bc}}\quad\cos\frac B2=\sqrt{\frac{s(s-b)}{ca}}\quad\cos\frac C2=\sqrt{\frac{s(s-c)}{ab}}\]\[\tan\frac A2=\sqrt{\frac{(s-b)(s-c)}{s(s-a)}}=\frac{\Delta}{s(s-a)}\]\[\sin A=\frac2{bc}\sqrt{s(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)}=\frac{2\Delta}{bc}\]

Area of a triangle \((\Delta)\)

\[\Delta=\tfrac12ab\sin C=\tfrac12bc\sin A=\tfrac12ca\sin B=\sqrt{s(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)}\]

\(m\)-\(n\) rule

If \(D\) is on \(BC\) with \(BD:DC=m:n\), \(\angle BAD=\alpha\), \(\angle DAC=\beta\) and \(\angle ADC=\theta\), then

\[(m+n)\cot\theta=m\cot\alpha-n\cot\beta=n\cot B-m\cot C\]

Radius of the circumcircle

\[R=\frac a{2\sin A}=\frac b{2\sin B}=\frac c{2\sin C}=\frac{abc}{4\Delta}\]

Radius of the incircle

\[r=\frac\Delta s=(s-a)\tan\frac A2=(s-b)\tan\frac B2=(s-c)\tan\frac C2\]\[r=4R\sin\frac A2\sin\frac B2\sin\frac C2\]

Radii of the ex-circles

\[r_1=\frac\Delta{s-a},\quad r_2=\frac\Delta{s-b},\quad r_3=\frac\Delta{s-c}\]\[r_1=s\tan\frac A2,\quad r_2=s\tan\frac B2,\quad r_3=s\tan\frac C2\]\[r_1=4R\sin\frac A2\cos\frac B2\cos\frac C2\]

Angle bisector, median and altitude from \(A\)

\[\beta_a=\frac{2bc\cos\frac A2}{b+c}\qquad m_a=\tfrac12\sqrt{2b^2+2c^2-a^2}\qquad A_a=\frac{2\Delta}a\]

\(\beta_a\) is the length of the angle bisector, \(m_a\) the median and \(A_a\) the altitude from \(A\).

Orthocentre and pedal triangle

The triangle \(KLM\) formed by joining the feet of the altitudes is the pedal triangle.

  • Its angles are \(\pi-2A,\ \pi-2B\) and \(\pi-2C\).
  • Its sides are \(a\cos A=R\sin2A\), \(b\cos B=R\sin2B\) and \(c\cos C=R\sin2C\).
  • The circumradii of triangles \(PBC,PCA,PAB\) and \(ABC\) are equal.

Excentral triangle

The triangle formed by joining the three excentres \(I_1,I_2,I_3\) of \(\triangle ABC\).

  • \(\triangle ABC\) is the pedal triangle of \(\triangle I_1I_2I_3\).
  • Its angles are \(\dfrac\pi2-\dfrac A2,\ \dfrac\pi2-\dfrac B2\) and \(\dfrac\pi2-\dfrac C2\).
  • Its sides are \(4R\cos\dfrac A2,\ 4R\cos\dfrac B2\) and \(4R\cos\dfrac C2\).
  • \(II_1=4R\sin\dfrac A2,\ II_2=4R\sin\dfrac B2,\ II_3=4R\sin\dfrac C2\).
  • The incentre \(I\) of \(\triangle ABC\) is the orthocentre of the excentral \(\triangle I_1I_2I_3\).

Distance between special points

\[OH^2=R^2(1-8\cos A\cos B\cos C)\quad\text{(circumcentre to orthocentre)}\]\[OI^2=R^2\left(1-8\sin\tfrac A2\sin\tfrac B2\sin\tfrac C2\right)=R^2-2Rr\quad\text{(circumcentre to incentre)}\]\[OG^2=R^2-\tfrac19(a^2+b^2+c^2)\quad\text{(circumcentre to centroid)}\]

Identities when \(A+B+C=\pi\)

\[\sin A+\sin B+\sin C=4\cos\tfrac A2\cos\tfrac B2\cos\tfrac C2,\qquad\cos A+\cos B+\cos C=1+4\sin\tfrac A2\sin\tfrac B2\sin\tfrac C2\]\[\sin2A+\sin2B+\sin2C=4\sin A\sin B\sin C,\qquad\cos2A+\cos2B+\cos2C=-1-4\cos A\cos B\cos C\]\[\sin^2A+\sin^2B+\sin^2C=2+2\cos A\cos B\cos C,\qquad\cos^2A+\cos^2B+\cos^2C=1-2\cos A\cos B\cos C\]\[\tan A+\tan B+\tan C=\tan A\tan B\tan C,\qquad\cot A\cot B+\cot B\cot C+\cot C\cot A=1\]\[\tan\tfrac A2\tan\tfrac B2+\tan\tfrac B2\tan\tfrac C2+\tan\tfrac C2\tan\tfrac A2=1,\qquad\cot\tfrac A2+\cot\tfrac B2+\cot\tfrac C2=\cot\tfrac A2\cot\tfrac B2\cot\tfrac C2\]
  • \(\sin(B+C)=\sin A\), \(\cos(B+C)=-\cos A\), \(\sin\dfrac{B+C}2=\cos\dfrac A2\), \(\tan\dfrac{B+C}2=\cot\dfrac A2\).
  • Maximum values (any triangle): \(\sin A+\sin B+\sin C\le\dfrac{3\sqrt3}2\); \(\sin A\sin B\sin C\le\dfrac{3\sqrt3}8\); \(\cos A+\cos B+\cos C\le\dfrac32\); \(\cos A\cos B\cos C\le\dfrac18\); \(\sin\frac A2\sin\frac B2\sin\frac C2\le\dfrac18\); in an acute triangle \(\tan A+\tan B+\tan C\ge3\sqrt3\). Equality only for the equilateral triangle.
  • Right-angled iff \(\sin^2A+\sin^2B+\sin^2C=2\) iff \(\cos^2A+\cos^2B+\cos^2C=1\) (one angle \(=90^\circ\)).

Relations among \(R,\ r,\ s,\ \Delta\) and the exradii

\[\Delta=rs=\frac{abc}{4R}=\sqrt{s(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)}=\sqrt{rr_1r_2r_3},\qquad abc=4Rrs\]\[r_1+r_2+r_3=4R+r,\qquad r_1r_2+r_2r_3+r_3r_1=s^2,\qquad\frac1r=\frac1{r_1}+\frac1{r_2}+\frac1{r_3},\qquad r_1r_2r_3=s\Delta\]\[a^2+b^2+c^2=2(s^2-r^2-4Rr),\qquad ab+bc+ca=s^2+r^2+4Rr\]\[s=4R\cos\tfrac A2\cos\tfrac B2\cos\tfrac C2,\qquad\cot\tfrac A2+\cot\tfrac B2+\cot\tfrac C2=\frac sr\]
  • \(\tan\dfrac A2=\dfrac r{s-a}=\dfrac{r_1}{s}\); \(a=r\left(\cot\dfrac B2+\cot\dfrac C2\right)\); \(a=r_1\left(\tan\dfrac B2+\tan\dfrac C2\right)\); \(r_1=\dfrac{a\cos\frac B2\cos\frac C2}{\cos\frac A2}\).
  • \(\tan A=\dfrac{4\Delta}{b^2+c^2-a^2}\), \(\cot A=\dfrac{b^2+c^2-a^2}{4\Delta}\); \(\cot A+\cot B+\cot C=\dfrac{a^2+b^2+c^2}{4\Delta}\).
  • Euler: \(R\ge2r\) (equality iff equilateral). Weitzenböck: \(a^2+b^2+c^2\ge4\sqrt3\,\Delta\). Also \(abc\ge8(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)\), \(s\ge3\sqrt3\,r\), \(\Delta\le\dfrac{s^2}{3\sqrt3}\).
  • Tangent lengths from the vertices to the incircle: \(s-a\) from \(A\), \(s-b\) from \(B\), \(s-c\) from \(C\). Tangent lengths to the \(A\)-excircle from \(A\): \(s\).

Mollweide, cotangent and other formulas

\[\frac{a-b}c=\frac{\sin\frac{A-B}2}{\cos\frac C2},\qquad\frac{a+b}c=\frac{\cos\frac{A-B}2}{\sin\frac C2}\]\[\frac{\cot\frac A2}{s-a}=\frac{\cot\frac B2}{s-b}=\frac{\cot\frac C2}{s-c}=\frac{s}{\Delta}\ \text{(law of cotangents)}\]\[a=2R\sin A,\quad b=2R\sin B,\quad c=2R\sin C;\qquad\Delta=2R^2\sin A\sin B\sin C=\frac{a^2\sin B\sin C}{2\sin A}\]
  • \(\sin A:\sin B:\sin C=a:b:c\); \(\Delta=\dfrac12r\,(a+b+c)\); \(\Delta=\dfrac14\sqrt{2a^2b^2+2b^2c^2+2c^2a^2-a^4-b^4-c^4}\).
  • \(a=b\cos C+c\cos B\) etc. Area with three sides and \(R\): \(\Delta=\dfrac{abc}{4R}\). Area in terms of altitudes \(h_a,h_b,h_c\): \(\dfrac1{\Delta}=\sqrt{\left(\dfrac1{h_a}+\dfrac1{h_b}+\dfrac1{h_c}\right)\left(-\dfrac1{h_a}+\dfrac1{h_b}+\dfrac1{h_c}\right)\left(\dfrac1{h_a}-\dfrac1{h_b}+\dfrac1{h_c}\right)\left(\dfrac1{h_a}+\dfrac1{h_b}-\dfrac1{h_c}\right)}\); and \(\dfrac1r=\dfrac1{h_a}+\dfrac1{h_b}+\dfrac1{h_c}\).

Ambiguous case (SSA): given \(a,b,A\)

\(\sin B=\dfrac{b\sin A}a\). The side \(c\) satisfies \(c^2-2bc\cos A+b^2-a^2=0\), so the two possible values have sum \(2b\cos A\) and product \(b^2-a^2\).

\(A\)ConditionNumber of triangles
acute\(a\lt b\sin A\)none
\(a=b\sin A\)one (right-angled at \(B\))
\(b\sin A\lt a\lt b\)two (\(B\) acute or obtuse)
\(a\ge b\)one
obtuse or right\(a\gt b\)one
\(a\le b\)none

SSS, SAS, ASA and AAS always give a unique triangle (when it exists). Solve with the cosine rule for SSS/SAS and the sine rule for the angle-side cases.

Special triangles

  • Right-angled (\(C=90^\circ\)): \(R=\dfrac c2\); \(r=\dfrac{a+b-c}2=s-c\); \(\Delta=\dfrac12ab\); \(r_3=s\); \(r_1=s-b\), \(r_2=s-a\).
  • Equilateral (side \(a\)): \(\Delta=\dfrac{\sqrt3}4a^2\); height \(\dfrac{\sqrt3}2a\); \(R=\dfrac a{\sqrt3}\); \(r=\dfrac a{2\sqrt3}\); \(r_1=\dfrac{\sqrt3}2a\); \(R=2r\).
  • Isosceles (\(a=b\)): \(\Delta=\dfrac12a^2\sin C\); \(R=\dfrac c{2\sin C}\).
  • Sides in A.P. (\(2b=a+c\)): \(\cot\frac A2,\cot\frac B2,\cot\frac C2\) are in A.P.; \(\tan\frac A2\tan\frac C2=\dfrac13\); \(\sin A+\sin C=2\sin B\); \(r=\dfrac{\Delta}{3b/2}\); \(b\) is the mean of the other two sides.
  • \(a\cos A+b\cos B+c\cos C=R(\sin2A+\sin2B+\sin2C)=4R\sin A\sin B\sin C\) (the perimeter of the pedal triangle is \(\dfrac{2\Delta}R\)).
  • Medial triangle (midpoints of sides): area \(\dfrac\Delta4\), circumradius \(\dfrac R2\); it is similar to \(\triangle ABC\).
  • \(a\cos B=b\cos A\iff a=b\) (isosceles); \(\triangle\) is right-angled at \(C\) iff \(a^2+b^2=c^2\) iff \(R=\dfrac c2\) iff \(\cos A=\dfrac bc\).

Cevians, medians and distances

  • Medians: \(m_a^2=\dfrac{2b^2+2c^2-a^2}4\); \(\sum m_a^2=\dfrac34\sum a^2\); the centroid divides each median \(2:1\); area of the triangle formed by the medians is \(\dfrac34\Delta\).
  • Angle bisector from \(A\) (meeting \(BC\) at \(D\)): \(BD:DC=c:b\); length \(\dfrac{2bc\cos\frac A2}{b+c}=\sqrt{bc\left[1-\dfrac{a^2}{(b+c)^2}\right]}\).
  • Stewart’s theorem: for a cevian \(AD\) with \(BD=m\), \(DC=n\), \(BC=a\): \(b^2m+c^2n=a(d^2+mn)\).
  • \(AH=2R\cos A\); distance of \(O\) from \(BC\) is \(R\cos A\); \(AH=2\times\) (distance of \(O\) from \(BC\)); \(AI=\dfrac r{\sin\frac A2}=4R\sin\dfrac B2\sin\dfrac C2\); \(AO=R\).
  • The reflection of \(H\) in a side, and in the midpoint of that side, lies on the circumcircle. The feet of the altitude and median from \(A\) are \(\left|\dfrac{b^2-c^2}{2a}\right|\) apart.
  • Altitudes: \(h_a=b\sin C=c\sin B=\dfrac{2\Delta}a\); \(h_a:h_b:h_c=\dfrac1a:\dfrac1b:\dfrac1c\).

Quadrilaterals and regular polygons

  • Cyclic quadrilateral (sides \(a,b,c,d\), \(s=\tfrac{a+b+c+d}2\)): area \(\sqrt{(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)(s-d)}\); opposite angles sum to \(\pi\); Ptolemy \(pq=ac+bd\); diagonals \(p^2=\dfrac{(ac+bd)(ad+bc)}{ab+cd}\), \(q^2=\dfrac{(ac+bd)(ab+cd)}{ad+bc}\); circumradius \(R=\dfrac14\sqrt{\dfrac{(ab+cd)(ac+bd)(ad+bc)}{(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)(s-d)}}\).
  • General quadrilateral (Bretschneider): \(\text{area}=\sqrt{(s-a)(s-b)(s-c)(s-d)-abcd\cos^2\frac{A+C}2}\); with diagonals \(p,q\) at angle \(\theta\): \(\tfrac12pq\sin\theta\).
  • Tangential quadrilateral (has an incircle): \(a+c=b+d\); area \(=r\,s\).
  • Regular \(n\)-gon of side \(a\): circumradius \(\dfrac a{2\sin\frac\pi n}\); inradius \(\dfrac a2\cot\dfrac\pi n\); area \(\dfrac{na^2}4\cot\dfrac\pi n=\dfrac n2R^2\sin\dfrac{2\pi}n=nr^2\tan\dfrac\pi n\); interior angle \(\pi-\dfrac{2\pi}n\); perimeter \(na\).
29
Trigonometry

Inverse Trigonometric Functions

Principal values and domains

FunctionDomainRange
\(y=\sin^{-1}x\)\(-1\le x\le1\)\(-\frac\pi2\le y\le\frac\pi2\)
\(y=\cos^{-1}x\)\(-1\le x\le1\)\(0\le y\le\pi\)
\(y=\tan^{-1}x\)\(x\in R\)\(-\frac\pi2\lt y\lt\frac\pi2\)
\(y=\csc^{-1}x\)\(x\le-1\) or \(x\ge1\)\(-\frac\pi2\le y\le\frac\pi2,\ y\ne0\)
\(y=\sec^{-1}x\)\(x\le-1\) or \(x\ge1\)\(0\le y\le\pi;\ y\ne\frac\pi2\)
\(y=\cot^{-1}x\)\(x\in R\)\(0\lt y\lt\pi\)

P-2: composition of function and inverse

\[\sin^{-1}(\sin x)=x,\ -\tfrac\pi2\le x\le\tfrac\pi2\qquad\cos^{-1}(\cos x)=x,\ 0\le x\le\pi\]\[\tan^{-1}(\tan x)=x,\ -\tfrac\pi2\lt x\lt\tfrac\pi2\qquad\cot^{-1}(\cot x)=x,\ 0\lt x\lt\pi\]\[\sec^{-1}(\sec x)=x,\ 0\le x\le\pi,\ x\ne\tfrac\pi2\qquad\csc^{-1}(\csc x)=x,\ x\ne0,\ -\tfrac\pi2\le x\le\tfrac\pi2\]

P-3: negative arguments

\[\sin^{-1}(-x)=-\sin^{-1}x,\ -1\le x\le1\qquad\tan^{-1}(-x)=-\tan^{-1}x,\ x\in R\]\[\cos^{-1}(-x)=\pi-\cos^{-1}x,\ -1\le x\le1\qquad\cot^{-1}(-x)=\pi-\cot^{-1}x,\ x\in R\]

P-5: complementary pairs

\[\sin^{-1}x+\cos^{-1}x=\frac\pi2,\ -1\le x\le1\]\[\tan^{-1}x+\cot^{-1}x=\frac\pi2,\ x\in R\]\[\csc^{-1}x+\sec^{-1}x=\frac\pi2,\ |x|\ge1\]

I-1: identities of addition

\[\sin^{-1}x+\sin^{-1}y=\begin{cases}\sin^{-1}\!\left[x\sqrt{1-y^2}+y\sqrt{1-x^2}\right],&x\ge0,\ y\ge0\ \&\ (x^2+y^2)\le1\\[4pt]\pi-\sin^{-1}\!\left[x\sqrt{1-y^2}+y\sqrt{1-x^2}\right],&x\ge0,\ y\ge0\ \&\ x^2+y^2\gt1\end{cases}\]\[\cos^{-1}x+\cos^{-1}y=\cos^{-1}\!\left[xy-\sqrt{1-x^2}\sqrt{1-y^2}\right],\quad x\ge0,\ y\ge0\]\[\tan^{-1}x+\tan^{-1}y=\begin{cases}\tan^{-1}\dfrac{x+y}{1-xy},&x\gt0,\ y\gt0\ \&\ xy\lt1\\[8pt]\pi+\tan^{-1}\dfrac{x+y}{1-xy},&x\gt0,\ y\gt0\ \&\ xy\gt1\\[8pt]\dfrac\pi2,&x\gt0,\ y\gt0\ \&\ xy=1\end{cases}\]

I-2: identities of subtraction

\[\sin^{-1}x-\sin^{-1}y=\sin^{-1}\!\left[x\sqrt{1-y^2}-y\sqrt{1-x^2}\right],\quad x\ge0,\ y\ge0\]\[\cos^{-1}x-\cos^{-1}y=\cos^{-1}\!\left[xy+\sqrt{1-x^2}\sqrt{1-y^2}\right],\quad x\ge0,\ y\ge0,\ x\le y\]\[\tan^{-1}x-\tan^{-1}y=\tan^{-1}\frac{x-y}{1+xy},\quad x\ge0,\ y\ge0\]

I-3: double-angle forms

\[\sin^{-1}\!\left(2x\sqrt{1-x^2}\right)=\begin{cases}2\sin^{-1}x,&|x|\le\frac1{\sqrt2}\\\pi-2\sin^{-1}x,&x\gt\frac1{\sqrt2}\\-(\pi+2\sin^{-1}x),&x\lt-\frac1{\sqrt2}\end{cases}\]\[\cos^{-1}(2x^2-1)=\begin{cases}2\cos^{-1}x,&0\le x\le1\\2\pi-2\cos^{-1}x,&-1\le x\lt0\end{cases}\]\[\tan^{-1}\frac{2x}{1-x^2}=\begin{cases}2\tan^{-1}x,&|x|\lt1\\\pi+2\tan^{-1}x,&x\lt-1\\-(\pi-2\tan^{-1}x),&x\gt1\end{cases}\]\[\sin^{-1}\frac{2x}{1+x^2}=\begin{cases}2\tan^{-1}x,&|x|\le1\\\pi-2\tan^{-1}x,&x\gt1\\-(\pi+2\tan^{-1}x),&x\lt-1\end{cases}\]\[\cos^{-1}\frac{1-x^2}{1+x^2}=\begin{cases}2\tan^{-1}x,&x\ge0\\-2\tan^{-1}x,&x\lt0\end{cases}\]

Three-term identity and notes

\[\tan^{-1}x+\tan^{-1}y+\tan^{-1}z=\tan^{-1}\!\left[\frac{x+y+z-xyz}{1-xy-yz-zx}\right]\quad\text{if }x,y,z\gt0\ \&\ (xy+yz+zx)\lt1\]
  • If \(\tan^{-1}x+\tan^{-1}y+\tan^{-1}z=\pi\), then \(x+y+z=xyz\).
  • If \(\tan^{-1}x+\tan^{-1}y+\tan^{-1}z=\dfrac\pi2\), then \(xy+yz+zx=1\).
  • \(\tan^{-1}1+\tan^{-1}2+\tan^{-1}3=\pi\)

Behaviour of the inverse functions

FunctionMonotonicityParity / symmetryBounds and special values
\(\sin^{-1}x\)increasingodd\(\sin^{-1}(\pm1)=\pm\frac\pi2\), \(\sin^{-1}0=0\)
\(\cos^{-1}x\)decreasing\(\cos^{-1}(-x)=\pi-\cos^{-1}x\)\(\cos^{-1}1=0\), \(\cos^{-1}(-1)=\pi\)
\(\tan^{-1}x\)increasingoddasymptotes \(y=\pm\frac\pi2\)
\(\cot^{-1}x\)decreasing\(\cot^{-1}(-x)=\pi-\cot^{-1}x\)asymptotes \(y=0,\ y=\pi\)
\(\sec^{-1}x\)increasing on each of \((-\infty,-1]\) and \([1,\infty)\)\(\sec^{-1}(-x)=\pi-\sec^{-1}x\)asymptote \(y=\frac\pi2\)
\(\operatorname{cosec}^{-1}x\)decreasing on each branchoddasymptote \(y=0\)
  • For \(|x|\le1\): \(\sin^{-1}x+\cos^{-1}x=\dfrac\pi2\), so \(a\sin^{-1}x+b\cos^{-1}x\) is linear in \(\sin^{-1}x\) — find its range from \(\sin^{-1}x\in[-\tfrac\pi2,\tfrac\pi2]\).
  • \(\sin^{-1}x\gt\cos^{-1}x\iff x\gt\dfrac1{\sqrt2}\); \(\ |\sin^{-1}x|\ge|x|\); \(\ |\tan^{-1}x|\le|x|\).
  • All six inverse trigonometric functions are one-one on their domains; the graph of \(y=\sin^{-1}x\) is the reflection of \(y=\sin x\) (on \([-\frac\pi2,\frac\pi2]\)) in the line \(y=x\), and similarly for the others.

\(f^{-1}(f(x))\) for all \(x\): the “sawtooth” forms

FunctionInterval of \(x\)Value
\(\sin^{-1}(\sin x)\)\([-\frac\pi2,\frac\pi2]\)\(x\)
\([\frac\pi2,\frac{3\pi}2]\)\(\pi-x\)
\([\frac{3\pi}2,\frac{5\pi}2]\)\(x-2\pi\)
\([-\frac{3\pi}2,-\frac\pi2]\)\(-\pi-x\)
\(\cos^{-1}(\cos x)\)\([-\pi,\pi]\)\(|x|\)
\([\pi,2\pi]\)\(2\pi-x\)
\([2\pi,3\pi]\)\(x-2\pi\)
\(\tan^{-1}(\tan x)\)\((-\frac\pi2,\frac\pi2)\)\(x\)
\((\frac\pi2,\frac{3\pi}2)\)\(x-\pi\)
\((-\frac{3\pi}2,-\frac\pi2)\)\(x+\pi\)

General statements: \(\sin^{-1}(\sin x)=(-1)^k(x-k\pi)\) for \(x\in\left[k\pi-\frac\pi2,k\pi+\frac\pi2\right]\); \(\tan^{-1}(\tan x)=x-k\pi\) for \(x\in\left(k\pi-\frac\pi2,k\pi+\frac\pi2\right)\); \(\cos^{-1}(\cos x)=|x-2k\pi|\) for \(x\in[(2k-1)\pi,(2k+1)\pi]\).

\(\sin^{-1}(\sin x)\) and \(\cos^{-1}(\cos x)\) are periodic with period \(2\pi\) (triangular waves with ranges \([-\frac\pi2,\frac\pi2]\) and \([0,\pi]\) respectively); \(\tan^{-1}(\tan x)\) has period \(\pi\). On the other hand \(\sin(\sin^{-1}x)=x\) and \(\cos(\cos^{-1}x)=x\) only for \(|x|\le1\), and \(\tan(\tan^{-1}x)=x\) for all real \(x\).

Trigonometric functions of inverse functions

ExpressionValueExpressionValue
\(\sin(\cos^{-1}x)\)\(\sqrt{1-x^2}\)\(\cos(\sin^{-1}x)\)\(\sqrt{1-x^2}\)
\(\tan(\sin^{-1}x)\)\(\dfrac x{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\)\(\tan(\cos^{-1}x)\)\(\dfrac{\sqrt{1-x^2}}x\)
\(\sin(\tan^{-1}x)\)\(\dfrac x{\sqrt{1+x^2}}\)\(\cos(\tan^{-1}x)\)\(\dfrac1{\sqrt{1+x^2}}\)
\(\sin(2\sin^{-1}x)\)\(2x\sqrt{1-x^2}\)\(\cos(2\sin^{-1}x)\)\(1-2x^2\)
\(\cos(2\cos^{-1}x)\)\(2x^2-1\)\(\tan(2\tan^{-1}x)\)\(\dfrac{2x}{1-x^2}\)

Conversion between inverse functions

\(\sin^{-1}\)\(\cos^{-1}\)\(\tan^{-1}\)
\(\sin^{-1}x=\)—\(\cos^{-1}\sqrt{1-x^2}\) (\(0\le x\le1\))\(\tan^{-1}\dfrac x{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\) (\(|x|\lt1\))
\(\cos^{-1}x=\)\(\sin^{-1}\sqrt{1-x^2}\) (\(0\le x\le1\))—\(\tan^{-1}\dfrac{\sqrt{1-x^2}}x\) (\(0\lt x\le1\))
\(\tan^{-1}x=\)\(\sin^{-1}\dfrac x{\sqrt{1+x^2}}\) (all \(x\))\(\cos^{-1}\dfrac1{\sqrt{1+x^2}}\) (\(x\ge0\))—
  • For negative arguments use \(\sin^{-1}(-x)=-\sin^{-1}x\), \(\cos^{-1}(-x)=\pi-\cos^{-1}x\), \(\tan^{-1}(-x)=-\tan^{-1}x\).
  • Reciprocals: \(\sin^{-1}\dfrac1x=\operatorname{cosec}^{-1}x\), \(\cos^{-1}\dfrac1x=\sec^{-1}x\) (\(|x|\ge1\)); \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac1x=\begin{cases}\cot^{-1}x&x\gt0\\\cot^{-1}x-\pi&x\lt0\end{cases}\); \(\tan^{-1}x+\tan^{-1}\dfrac1x=\pm\dfrac\pi2\) (sign of \(x\)).

Complete addition and subtraction rules for \(\tan^{-1}\)

\[\tan^{-1}x+\tan^{-1}y=\begin{cases}\tan^{-1}\dfrac{x+y}{1-xy},&xy\lt1\\[6pt]\pi+\tan^{-1}\dfrac{x+y}{1-xy},&xy\gt1,\ x\gt0\\[6pt]-\pi+\tan^{-1}\dfrac{x+y}{1-xy},&xy\gt1,\ x\lt0\end{cases}\]\[\tan^{-1}x-\tan^{-1}y=\begin{cases}\tan^{-1}\dfrac{x-y}{1+xy},&xy\gt-1\\[6pt]\pi+\tan^{-1}\dfrac{x-y}{1+xy},&xy\lt-1,\ x\gt0\\[6pt]-\pi+\tan^{-1}\dfrac{x-y}{1+xy},&xy\lt-1,\ x\lt0\end{cases}\]
  • If \(xy=1\): \(\tan^{-1}x+\tan^{-1}y=\pm\dfrac\pi2\) (sign of \(x\)).
  • \(\tan^{-1}x+\tan^{-1}\dfrac{1-x}{1+x}=\dfrac\pi4\) for \(x\gt-1\).
  • \(\sin^{-1}x\pm\sin^{-1}y\) and \(\cos^{-1}x\pm\cos^{-1}y\): use \(\sin(\sin^{-1}x\pm\sin^{-1}y)=x\sqrt{1-y^2}\pm y\sqrt{1-x^2}\) and choose the branch by the range of the left side.

Multiple-angle and substitution forms

\[3\sin^{-1}x=\sin^{-1}(3x-4x^3),\ |x|\le\tfrac12;\qquad3\cos^{-1}x=\cos^{-1}(4x^3-3x),\ \tfrac12\le x\le1\]\[3\tan^{-1}x=\tan^{-1}\frac{3x-x^3}{1-3x^2},\ |x|\lt\tfrac1{\sqrt3}\]
  • \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac{\sqrt{1+x^2}-1}x=\dfrac12\tan^{-1}x\); \(\tan^{-1}\left(\sqrt{1+x^2}-x\right)=\dfrac\pi4-\dfrac12\tan^{-1}x\); \(\tan^{-1}\left(\sqrt{1+x^2}+x\right)=\dfrac\pi4+\dfrac12\tan^{-1}x\).
  • \(\tan^{-1}\sqrt{\dfrac{1-\cos x}{1+\cos x}}=\dfrac{|x|}2\) for \(x\in(-\pi,\pi)\); \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac{\cos x}{1+\sin x}=\dfrac\pi4-\dfrac x2\) for \(x\in(-\tfrac\pi2,\tfrac{3\pi}2)\); \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac{\cos x-\sin x}{\cos x+\sin x}=\dfrac\pi4-x\) for \(x\in(-\tfrac\pi4,\tfrac{3\pi}4)\).
  • Standard substitutions: \(x=\sin\theta\) for \(\sqrt{1-x^2}\); \(x=\tan\theta\) for \(\sqrt{1+x^2}\) and \(1+x^2\); \(x=\cos2\theta\) for \(\sqrt{\dfrac{1-x}{1+x}}\); \(x=\sec\theta\) for \(\sqrt{x^2-1}\); \(x=a\sin\theta\) for \(\sqrt{a^2-x^2}\); \(x^2=a^2\cos2\theta\) for \(\sqrt{\dfrac{a^2-x^2}{a^2+x^2}}\).

Special values and standard sums

  • \(\sin^{-1}\dfrac12=\dfrac\pi6\), \(\sin^{-1}\dfrac{\sqrt2}2=\dfrac\pi4\), \(\sin^{-1}\dfrac{\sqrt3}2=\dfrac\pi3\); \(\cos^{-1}\dfrac12=\dfrac\pi3\), \(\cos^{-1}\left(-\dfrac12\right)=\dfrac{2\pi}3\), \(\cos^{-1}\left(-\dfrac{\sqrt3}2\right)=\dfrac{5\pi}6\); \(\tan^{-1}1=\dfrac\pi4\), \(\tan^{-1}\sqrt3=\dfrac\pi3\), \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac1{\sqrt3}=\dfrac\pi6\).
  • \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac12+\tan^{-1}\dfrac13=\dfrac\pi4\); \(\tan^{-1}2+\tan^{-1}3=\dfrac{3\pi}4\); \(2\tan^{-1}\dfrac12=\tan^{-1}\dfrac43\); \(2\tan^{-1}\dfrac13=\tan^{-1}\dfrac34\); \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac13+\tan^{-1}\dfrac15+\tan^{-1}\dfrac17+\tan^{-1}\dfrac18=\dfrac\pi4\); Machin: \(4\tan^{-1}\dfrac15-\tan^{-1}\dfrac1{239}=\dfrac\pi4\).
  • Telescoping: \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac{1}{1+n+n^2}=\tan^{-1}(n+1)-\tan^{-1}n\); hence \(\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^N\tan^{-1}\frac1{n^2+n+1}=\tan^{-1}(N+1)-\frac\pi4\). Also \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac1{2n^2}=\tan^{-1}(2n+1)-\tan^{-1}(2n-1)\), so \(\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^\infty\tan^{-1}\frac1{2n^2}=\frac\pi4\).
  • \(\displaystyle\sin^{-1}x+\sin^{-1}y+\sin^{-1}z=\frac{3\pi}2\) forces \(x=y=z=1\); \(\cos^{-1}x+\cos^{-1}y+\cos^{-1}z=3\pi\) forces \(x=y=z=-1\) (extreme-value arguments).
30
Trigonometry

Heights & Distances

Angles of elevation and depression

  • The angle of elevation of an object is the angle between the horizontal through the observer’s eye and the line of sight up to the object; the angle of depression is the angle below the horizontal to an object lower than the observer.
  • The angle of elevation of \(B\) from \(A\) equals the angle of depression of \(A\) from \(B\) (alternate angles).
  • For a vertical object of height \(h\) at horizontal distance \(d\): \(\tan\theta=\dfrac hd\), i.e. \(h=d\tan\theta\), \(d=h\cot\theta\); slant distance \(=\dfrac h{\sin\theta}\).
  • Shadow of a vertical pole of height \(h\) when the sun’s altitude is \(\theta\): \(\ell=h\cot\theta\). If the shadow length equals the height, the sun’s altitude is \(45^\circ\).
  • Always draw a clear figure: mark the horizontal ground line, the vertical object, the observer’s eye level, and every given angle.

Two observations along a line through the foot

A tower \(PQ\) of height \(h\); an observer at \(A\) measures elevation \(\alpha\) and, after moving a distance \(d\) in line with the foot \(Q\), measures \(\beta\).

\[\text{Moving towards the tower }(\beta\gt\alpha):\quad h=\frac d{\cot\alpha-\cot\beta}=\frac{d\sin\alpha\sin\beta}{\sin(\beta-\alpha)}\]\[\text{Points on opposite sides of the tower:}\quad h=\frac d{\cot\alpha+\cot\beta}=\frac{d\sin\alpha\sin\beta}{\sin(\alpha+\beta)}\]
  • The distance of the nearer point from the foot: \(h\cot\beta\); of the farther point: \(h\cot\alpha\).
  • If the elevations at points \(a\) and \(b\) from the foot on the same line are complementary, the height is \(h=\sqrt{ab}\) (since \(\tan\alpha\tan(90^\circ-\alpha)=1\)).
  • If the angle of elevation is \(\theta\) at the start and becomes \(2\theta\) after walking \(d\) towards the tower, then the height is \(d\sin2\theta\) (isosceles triangle with \(AB=BP=d\)).
  • Elevation \(30^\circ\) then \(60^\circ\): \(h=\dfrac{d\sqrt3}2\), i.e. \(d=\dfrac{2h}{\sqrt3}\); elevation \(45^\circ\) then \(60^\circ\): \(h=\dfrac d{1-1/\sqrt3}=\dfrac{d(3+\sqrt3)}2\); elevation \(30^\circ\) then \(45^\circ\): \(h=\dfrac{d}{\sqrt3-1}=\dfrac{d(\sqrt3+1)}2\).

Tower with a flagstaff; object of finite height

  • A flagstaff of length \(\ell\) on a tower of height \(h\), seen from a point at distance \(d\): \(\tan\alpha=\dfrac hd\) (base of staff) and \(\tan\beta=\dfrac{h+\ell}d\) (top). Eliminating \(d\): \(h=\dfrac{\ell\tan\alpha}{\tan\beta-\tan\alpha}\).
  • A vertical object between heights \(a\) and \(b\) (above the eye level) subtends the angle \(\theta\) at a horizontal distance \(x\): \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{(b-a)x}{x^2+ab}\); it is greatest at \(x=\sqrt{ab}\), where \(\tan\theta_{\max}=\dfrac{b-a}{2\sqrt{ab}}\) (best viewing distance).
  • A pole of length \(\ell\) on top of a tower of height \(h\) subtends equal angles at two points on the ground at distances \(p\) and \(q\) from the foot: \(pq=h(h+\ell)\).
  • For a hill or cliff, use the sine rule in the triangle formed by the two observation points and the summit to get the slant distance, then multiply by \(\sin\) of the elevation.

Sine and cosine rules in height problems

  • If the two observation points \(A\), \(B\) and the foot \(Q\) are not collinear, find \(AB=d\) and the angle \(\angle AQB=\gamma\) from the map; then \(AQ=h\cot\alpha\), \(BQ=h\cot\beta\) and \(d^2=h^2(\cot^2\alpha+\cot^2\beta-2\cot\alpha\cot\beta\cos\gamma)\).
  • Three observation points at the vertices of a triangle, elevations \(\alpha,\beta,\gamma\): the foot is at distances \(h\cot\alpha,h\cot\beta,h\cot\gamma\) from them. If the three elevations are equal, the foot is the circumcentre of the triangle of observation points.
  • For a tower on an inclined plane (slope \(\phi\)): draw the triangle (observer, foot of the tower, top of the tower), compute each of its angles from the horizontal and the slope (the tower is vertical, so it makes \(90^\circ+\phi\) or \(90^\circ-\phi\) with the slope), and apply the sine rule.
  • Elevations are always measured from the horizontal, even when the observer stands on a slope.

Directions and bearings

  • Take \(x\) east and \(y\) north. A displacement of \(d\) in direction N\(\theta\)E (bearing \(\theta\) east of north) is \((d\sin\theta,\ d\cos\theta)\); N\(\theta\)W is \((-d\sin\theta,\ d\cos\theta)\); S\(\theta\)E is \((d\sin\theta,\ -d\cos\theta)\); S\(\theta\)W is \((-d\sin\theta,\ -d\cos\theta)\).
  • North-east = N\(45^\circ\)E; the eight principal directions are \(45^\circ\) apart; the angle between N\(\theta_1\)E and N\(\theta_2\)E is \(|\theta_1-\theta_2|\).
  • A bearing (true bearing) is measured clockwise from north, \(0^\circ\) to \(360^\circ\): east \(=90^\circ\), south \(=180^\circ\), west \(=270^\circ\). Back-bearing \(=\) bearing \(\pm180^\circ\).
  • Ship problems: if a ship sails on a straight course at speed \(v\), the positions after time \(t\) are \(P_0+vt(\sin\theta,\cos\theta)\); closest approach to a fixed point \(O\) is the perpendicular distance from \(O\) to the course.
  • If a ship sailing along a straight line sees a lighthouse at bearings differing by \(\alpha\) between two positions \(d\) apart, apply the sine rule in the triangle of the two positions and the lighthouse.

Moving objects and reflections

  • An aeroplane at constant height \(h\) flying horizontally at speed \(v\), seen at elevations \(\alpha\) then \(\beta\) after time \(t\): \(vt=h|\cot\beta-\cot\alpha|\), so \(h=\dfrac{vt}{|\cot\beta-\cot\alpha|}\) (same side), or \(h=\dfrac{vt}{\cot\alpha+\cot\beta}\) (opposite sides).
  • Object receding from the foot of a vertical pole with speed \(u\): \(x=h\cot\theta\), \(\dfrac{dx}{dt}=-h\operatorname{cosec}^2\theta\,\dfrac{d\theta}{dt}\); the shadow of a person of height \(\ell\) walking from a lamp post of height \(L\) moves at \(\dfrac{L}{L-\ell}\) times the person’s speed.
  • Cloud and its reflection in a lake: an observer at height \(a\) above the water sees a cloud at elevation \(\alpha\) and its reflection at depression \(\beta\); the height of the cloud above the water is \(h=\dfrac{a(\tan\beta+\tan\alpha)}{\tan\beta-\tan\alpha}=\dfrac{a\sin(\beta+\alpha)}{\sin(\beta-\alpha)}\).
  • Angular size: an object of length \(\ell\) at distance \(r\) subtends \(\theta\approx\dfrac\ell r\) radians when \(\theta\) is small.

Useful trigonometric values for height problems

\(\theta\)\(15^\circ\)\(18^\circ\)\(22.5^\circ\)\(30^\circ\)\(36^\circ\)\(45^\circ\)\(60^\circ\)\(67.5^\circ\)\(75^\circ\)
\(\tan\theta\)\(2-\sqrt3\)\(\sqrt{1-\frac2{\sqrt5}}\)\(\sqrt2-1\)\(\dfrac1{\sqrt3}\)\(\sqrt{5-2\sqrt5}\)\(1\)\(\sqrt3\)\(\sqrt2+1\)\(2+\sqrt3\)
\(\sin\theta\)\(\frac{\sqrt6-\sqrt2}4\)\(\frac{\sqrt5-1}4\)\(\frac{\sqrt{2-\sqrt2}}2\)\(\frac12\)\(\frac{\sqrt{10-2\sqrt5}}4\)\(\frac1{\sqrt2}\)\(\frac{\sqrt3}2\)\(\frac{\sqrt{2+\sqrt2}}2\)\(\frac{\sqrt6+\sqrt2}4\)
\(\cos\theta\)\(\frac{\sqrt6+\sqrt2}4\)\(\frac{\sqrt{10+2\sqrt5}}4\)\(\frac{\sqrt{2+\sqrt2}}2\)\(\frac{\sqrt3}2\)\(\frac{\sqrt5+1}4\)\(\frac1{\sqrt2}\)\(\frac12\)\(\frac{\sqrt{2-\sqrt2}}2\)\(\frac{\sqrt6-\sqrt2}4\)

Ratios worth remembering: \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac12\approx26.57^\circ\), \(\tan^{-1}\dfrac34\approx36.87^\circ\), \(\tan^{-1}2\approx63.43^\circ\); 3-4-5 and 5-12-13 triangles.

31
Statistics & logic

Statistics

Arithmetic mean

For \(n\) values \(x_1,\ldots,x_n\):

\[\bar x=\frac{x_1+x_2+\cdots+x_n}n=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^nx_i}n\]

With frequencies \(f_1,\ldots,f_n\):

\[\bar x=\frac{f_1x_1+f_2x_2+\cdots+f_nx_n}{f_1+f_2+\cdots+f_n}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^nf_ix_i}N,\qquad N=\sum_{i=1}^nf_i\]

Properties of the arithmetic mean

  • The sum of deviations of the variate from the A.M. is always zero: \(\sum(x_i-\bar x)=0\).
  • The sum of squares of deviations is minimum when taken about the A.M.: \(\sum(x_i-\bar x)^2\) is minimum.
  • If \(\bar x\) is the mean of \(x_i\), then the A.M. of \((x_i+\lambda)=\bar x+\lambda\), of \(\lambda x_i=\lambda\bar x\), and of \((ax_i+b)=a\bar x+b\).

Median

The middle term of the series when values are arranged in ascending or descending order. For an ungrouped distribution with \(n\) variates:

\[\text{Median}=\begin{cases}\left(\dfrac{n+1}2\right)^{\text{th}}\text{ term},&n\text{ odd}\\[8pt]\text{mean of }\left(\dfrac n2\right)^{\text{th}}\text{ and }\left(\dfrac n2+1\right)^{\text{th}}\text{ term},&n\text{ even}\end{cases}\]

Mode

The value of the variate with the maximum frequency (for ungrouped data, and for an ungrouped frequency distribution).

  • In a symmetric distribution, mean \(=\) mode \(=\) median.
  • In a moderately asymmetrical distribution, the median divides mean and mode internally in the ratio 1 : 2:
\[\text{median}=\frac{2(\text{Mean})+(\text{Mode})}3\]

Range

\[\frac{\text{difference of extreme values}}{\text{sum of extreme values}}=\frac{L-S}{L+S}\]

\(L\) is the largest value and \(S\) the smallest.

Mean deviation about \(A\)

\[\text{M.D.}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^n|x_i-A|}n\qquad\text{M.D.}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^nf_i|x_i-A|}N\ \ \text{(frequency distribution)}\]

Variance and standard deviation

\[\text{S.D.}=+\sqrt{\text{variance}}\]\[\sigma_x^2=\frac{\sum(x_i-\bar x)^2}n=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^nx_i^2}n-\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^nx_i}n\right)^2=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^nx_i^2}n-(\bar x)^2\]\[\sigma_d^2=\frac{\sum d_i^2}n-\left(\frac{\sum d_i}n\right)^2,\qquad d_i=x_i-a,\ a=\text{assumed mean}\]\[\text{Coefficient of S.D.}=\frac\sigma{\bar x}\qquad\text{Coefficient of variation}=\frac\sigma{\bar x}\times100\ (\%)\]

Properties of variance (\(\lambda,a,b\) constants): \(\text{var}(x_i+\lambda)=\text{var}(x_i)\); \(\text{var}(\lambda x_i)=\lambda^2\text{var}(x_i)\); \(\text{var}(ax_i+b)=a^2\text{var}(x_i)\).

Grouped data: mean by three methods

Class mark \(x_i=\dfrac{\text{lower limit}+\text{upper limit}}2\); frequencies \(f_i\); \(N=\sum f_i\); class width \(h\).

\[\text{Direct: }\bar x=\frac{\sum f_ix_i}N\qquad\text{Assumed mean }a:\ \bar x=a+\frac{\sum f_id_i}N,\ d_i=x_i-a\qquad\text{Step deviation: }\bar x=a+h\,\frac{\sum f_iu_i}N,\ u_i=\frac{x_i-a}h\]\[\sigma^2=h^2\left[\frac{\sum f_iu_i^2}N-\left(\frac{\sum f_iu_i}N\right)^2\right]\]

Make classes continuous first (e.g. \(10\text{–}19,20\text{–}29\) become \(9.5\text{–}19.5,19.5\text{–}29.5\)) before computing median, quartiles or mode.

Median, quartiles and mode for grouped data

\[\text{Median}=l+\frac{\frac N2-C}{f}\times h,\qquad Q_1=l+\frac{\frac N4-C}{f}h,\qquad Q_3=l+\frac{\frac{3N}4-C}{f}h,\qquad P_k=l+\frac{\frac{kN}{100}-C}{f}h\]

\(l\) = lower boundary of the median (or quartile) class, \(C\) = cumulative frequency of the class before it, \(f\) = its frequency, \(h\) = class width.

\[\text{Mode}=l+\frac{f_1-f_0}{2f_1-f_0-f_2}\times h\]

\(l\) = lower boundary of the modal class (highest frequency), \(f_1\) its frequency, \(f_0\) and \(f_2\) the frequencies of the preceding and following classes.

  • Empirical relation: Mode \(=3\,\text{Median}-2\,\text{Mean}\). The median can also be read from the intersection of the less-than and more-than ogives.
  • Interquartile range \(=Q_3-Q_1\); quartile deviation \(=\dfrac{Q_3-Q_1}2\); coefficient of quartile deviation \(=\dfrac{Q_3-Q_1}{Q_3+Q_1}\).

Mean deviation and other measures of dispersion

\[\text{M.D.}(\bar x)=\frac{\sum f_i|x_i-\bar x|}N,\qquad\text{M.D.}(M)=\frac{\sum f_i|x_i-M|}N\ (M=\text{median}),\qquad\text{coefficient}=\frac{\text{M.D.}}{\text{mean or median}}\]
  • The sum \(\sum|x_i-A|\) is least when \(A\) is the median; \(\sum(x_i-A)^2\) is least when \(A\) is the mean.
  • \(\text{M.D.}\le\sigma\) (variance is at least the square of M.D. about the mean); \(\sigma\le\dfrac{\text{range}}2\) (equality for a two-point distribution with equal weights).
  • Range \(=L-S\); coefficient of range \(=\dfrac{L-S}{L+S}\); coefficient of variation \(\text{C.V.}=\dfrac\sigma{\bar x}\times100\). A smaller C.V. means a more consistent distribution.
  • Effect of a linear change \(y=ax+b\): \(\bar y=a\bar x+b\), \(\sigma_y=|a|\sigma_x\), M.D.\(_y=|a|\)M.D.\(_x\), range\(_y=|a|\) range\(_x\); adding a constant leaves all dispersion measures unchanged.

Combined mean and variance; corrected data

  • Two groups of sizes \(n_1,n_2\), means \(\bar x_1,\bar x_2\), variances \(\sigma_1^2,\sigma_2^2\):
\[\bar x=\frac{n_1\bar x_1+n_2\bar x_2}{n_1+n_2},\qquad\sigma^2=\frac{n_1(\sigma_1^2+d_1^2)+n_2(\sigma_2^2+d_2^2)}{n_1+n_2},\quad d_1=\bar x_1-\bar x,\ d_2=\bar x_2-\bar x\]
  • Corrected data: if a value \(x_{\text{wrong}}\) was recorded instead of \(x_{\text{right}}\), then \(\sum x_{\text{new}}=\sum x-x_{\text{wrong}}+x_{\text{right}}\) and \(\sum x^2_{\text{new}}=\sum x^2-x_{\text{wrong}}^2+x_{\text{right}}^2\); recompute the mean and then the variance \(=\dfrac{\sum x^2}n-\bar x^2\).
  • If one item \(a\) is replaced by \(b\): new mean \(=\bar x+\dfrac{b-a}n\). Adding an item equal to the mean leaves the mean unchanged but changes \(n\) in the variance: \(\sigma^2_{\text{new}}=\dfrac{n}{n+1}\sigma^2\).
  • Mean of \(n\) numbers is \(\bar x\): removing an item \(a\) gives \(\dfrac{n\bar x-a}{n-1}\).
  • Sum of squares of deviations \(\sum(x_i-A)^2=\sum(x_i-\bar x)^2+n(\bar x-A)^2\).

Standard sequences: mean and variance

DataMeanVariance
\(1,2,\ldots,n\)\(\dfrac{n+1}2\)\(\dfrac{n^2-1}{12}\)
First \(n\) odd numbers \(1,3,\ldots,2n-1\)\(n\)\(\dfrac{n^2-1}3\)
First \(n\) even numbers \(2,4,\ldots,2n\)\(n+1\)\(\dfrac{n^2-1}3\)
A.P. \(a,a+d,\ldots,a+(n-1)d\)\(a+\dfrac{(n-1)d}2\)\(\dfrac{d^2(n^2-1)}{12}\)
Squares \(1^2,2^2,\ldots,n^2\)\(\dfrac{(n+1)(2n+1)}6\)\(\dfrac{(n^2-1)(2n+1)(8n+11)}{180}\)
Two values \(a,b\) equally weighted\(\dfrac{a+b}2\)\(\dfrac{(a-b)^2}4\)
Binomial \(B(n,p)\)\(np\)\(npq\)

Means: A.M., G.M., H.M. and weighted mean

  • For positive numbers: A.M. \(=\dfrac{\sum x_i}n\ \ge\) G.M. \(=\left(\prod x_i\right)^{1/n}\ \ge\) H.M. \(=\dfrac n{\sum\frac1{x_i}}\), with equality iff all values are equal. For two numbers G.M.\(^2=\) A.M.\(\times\)H.M.
  • Weighted mean: \(\dfrac{\sum w_ix_i}{\sum w_i}\). Mean of a group formed from sub-groups is the weighted mean of the group means.
  • Root-mean-square: \(\sqrt{\dfrac{\sum x_i^2}n}\ge\) A.M.; \(\text{R.M.S.}^2=\sigma^2+\bar x^2\).
  • Graphical presentation: cumulative frequency (less-than / more-than type) and ogives, histogram (areas proportional to frequency), frequency polygon, bar chart, pie chart (sector angle \(=\dfrac{\text{value}}{\text{total}}\times360^\circ\)).

Skewness and moments (beyond the syllabus)

  • Karl Pearson’s coefficient of skewness: \(\dfrac{\text{Mean}-\text{Mode}}\sigma=\dfrac{3(\text{Mean}-\text{Median})}\sigma\); Bowley’s: \(\dfrac{Q_3+Q_1-2\,\text{Median}}{Q_3-Q_1}\).
  • Positively skewed: Mean \(\gt\) Median \(\gt\) Mode; negatively skewed: Mean \(\lt\) Median \(\lt\) Mode; symmetric: all equal.
  • \(r\)th central moment \(\mu_r=\dfrac{\sum f_i(x_i-\bar x)^r}N\); \(\mu_1=0\), \(\mu_2=\sigma^2\); \(\beta_1=\dfrac{\mu_3^2}{\mu_2^3}\), \(\beta_2=\dfrac{\mu_4}{\mu_2^2}\) (kurtosis; \(\beta_2=3\) for a normal curve).

Correlation and regression (beyond the syllabus)

\[\operatorname{cov}(x,y)=\frac{\sum xy}n-\bar x\bar y,\qquad r=\frac{\operatorname{cov}(x,y)}{\sigma_x\sigma_y},\qquad-1\le r\le1\]\[\text{Spearman rank correlation: }\rho=1-\frac{6\sum d_i^2}{n(n^2-1)}\]
  • Regression of \(y\) on \(x\): \(y-\bar y=r\dfrac{\sigma_y}{\sigma_x}(x-\bar x)\) (\(b_{yx}=r\dfrac{\sigma_y}{\sigma_x}\)); of \(x\) on \(y\): \(x-\bar x=r\dfrac{\sigma_x}{\sigma_y}(y-\bar y)\) (\(b_{xy}=r\dfrac{\sigma_x}{\sigma_y}\)).
  • \(b_{yx}b_{xy}=r^2\); both coefficients have the sign of \(r\); the lines meet at \((\bar x,\bar y)\). Angle between them: \(\tan\theta=\dfrac{1-r^2}{|r|}\cdot\dfrac{\sigma_x\sigma_y}{\sigma_x^2+\sigma_y^2}\). For \(r=\pm1\) the lines coincide; for \(r=0\) they are perpendicular.
  • \(r\) is unchanged by a change of origin and by a positive change of scale; \(r\) is the geometric mean of the two regression coefficients (with their common sign); \(\text{A.M. of }b_{yx},b_{xy}\ge|r|\).
32
Statistics & logic

Mathematical Reasoning

Truth table

Let \(p\) and \(q\) be statements.

\(p\)\(q\)\(p\wedge q\)\(p\vee q\)\(p\to q\)\(q\to p\)\(p\leftrightarrow q\)
TTTTTTT
TFFTFTF
FTFTTFF
FFFFTTT

Tautology

A statement that is true for all truth values of its components, denoted \(t\). Example: \(p\vee{\sim}p\).

\(p\)\({\sim}p\)\(p\vee{\sim}p\)
TFT
FTT

Fallacy

A statement that is false for all truth values of its components, denoted \(f\) or \(c\). Example: \(p\wedge{\sim}p\).

\(p\)\({\sim}p\)\(p\wedge{\sim}p\)
TFF
FTF

Negation of compound statements

Statement\(p\wedge q\)\(p\vee q\)\(p\to q\)\(p\Leftrightarrow q\)
Negation\(({\sim}p)\vee({\sim}q)\)\(({\sim}p)\wedge({\sim}q)\)\(p\wedge({\sim}q)\)\(p\Leftrightarrow{\sim}q\)

Contrapositive

Let \(p\Rightarrow q\). Then the contrapositive of \(p\Rightarrow q\) is

\[{\sim}q\Rightarrow{\sim}p\]

Statements, connectives and equivalent forms

  • A statement is a sentence that is either true or false, not both. Questions, commands, exclamations and open sentences (“\(x+2=5\)”) are not statements. Truth values: T, F.
  • \({\sim}p\) (not), \(p\wedge q\) (and), \(p\vee q\) (or, inclusive), \(p\to q\) (if \(p\) then \(q\)), \(p\leftrightarrow q\) (iff). With \(n\) statements the truth table has \(2^n\) rows; there are \(2^{2^n}\) different truth tables (e.g. \(16\) for two statements).
  • \(p\to q\) is false only when \(p\) is true and \(q\) is false.
  • Equivalent forms: \(p\to q\equiv{\sim}p\vee q\equiv{\sim}q\to{\sim}p\); \(p\leftrightarrow q\equiv(p\to q)\wedge(q\to p)\); \({\sim}(p\to q)\equiv p\wedge{\sim}q\); \({\sim}(p\leftrightarrow q)\equiv(p\wedge{\sim}q)\vee(q\wedge{\sim}p)\equiv p\leftrightarrow{\sim}q\).
  • For \(p\to q\): converse \(q\to p\); inverse \({\sim}p\to{\sim}q\); contrapositive \({\sim}q\to{\sim}p\). A conditional is equivalent to its contrapositive and its converse is equivalent to its inverse.
  • English phrases: “\(p\) only if \(q\)” \(=p\to q\); “\(p\) if \(q\)” \(=q\to p\); “\(p\) unless \(q\)” \(={\sim}q\to p\); “\(p\) is sufficient for \(q\)” \(=p\to q\); “\(p\) is necessary for \(q\)” \(=q\to p\); “\(p\) is necessary and sufficient for \(q\)” \(=p\leftrightarrow q\).

Laws of logic

LawStatement
Idempotent\(p\wedge p\equiv p\), \(p\vee p\equiv p\)
Commutative\(p\wedge q\equiv q\wedge p\), \(p\vee q\equiv q\vee p\)
Associative\((p\wedge q)\wedge r\equiv p\wedge(q\wedge r)\); same for \(\vee\)
Distributive\(p\wedge(q\vee r)\equiv(p\wedge q)\vee(p\wedge r)\); \(p\vee(q\wedge r)\equiv(p\vee q)\wedge(p\vee r)\)
De Morgan\({\sim}(p\wedge q)\equiv{\sim}p\vee{\sim}q\); \({\sim}(p\vee q)\equiv{\sim}p\wedge{\sim}q\)
Double negation\({\sim}({\sim}p)\equiv p\)
Identity\(p\wedge t\equiv p\), \(p\vee f\equiv p\); \(p\vee t\equiv t\), \(p\wedge f\equiv f\)
Complement\(p\vee{\sim}p\equiv t\), \(p\wedge{\sim}p\equiv f\)
Absorption\(p\vee(p\wedge q)\equiv p\), \(p\wedge(p\vee q)\equiv p\)

Standard tautologies and rules of inference

  • \(p\vee{\sim}p\); \({\sim}(p\wedge{\sim}p)\); \(p\to p\); \((p\wedge q)\to p\); \(p\to(p\vee q)\).
  • Modus ponens: \([p\wedge(p\to q)]\to q\). Modus tollens: \([(p\to q)\wedge{\sim}q]\to{\sim}p\).
  • Hypothetical syllogism: \([(p\to q)\wedge(q\to r)]\to(p\to r)\). Disjunctive syllogism: \([(p\vee q)\wedge{\sim}p]\to q\).
  • \((p\to q)\leftrightarrow({\sim}q\to{\sim}p)\); \((p\to q)\leftrightarrow({\sim}p\vee q)\); \([(p\to q)\wedge(q\to p)]\leftrightarrow(p\leftrightarrow q)\).
  • A compound statement that is always false is a contradiction/fallacy; one that is sometimes true and sometimes false is a contingency.
  • Exclusive or: \(p\oplus q\equiv(p\vee q)\wedge{\sim}(p\wedge q)\equiv(p\wedge{\sim}q)\vee({\sim}p\wedge q)\equiv{\sim}(p\leftrightarrow q)\).

Quantifiers and their negations

  • Universal quantifier \(\forall\) (“for all”), existential quantifier \(\exists\) (“there exists”).
  • \({\sim}\big(\forall x\,P(x)\big)\equiv\exists x\,{\sim}P(x)\); \({\sim}\big(\exists x\,P(x)\big)\equiv\forall x\,{\sim}P(x)\).
  • Nested: \({\sim}(\forall x\,\exists y\,P)\equiv\exists x\,\forall y\,{\sim}P\); the order of unlike quantifiers matters.
  • Negation of “all \(A\) are \(B\)”: “some \(A\) is not \(B\)”. Negation of “no \(A\) is \(B\)”: “some \(A\) is \(B\)”. Negation of “some \(A\) are \(B\)”: “no \(A\) is \(B\)”.
  • To disprove a universal statement give one counter-example; to prove an existential one give one example.
  • Negation of “\(p\) and \(q\) both”: “not \(p\) or not \(q\)”; of “\(x\gt a\)”: “\(x\le a\)”; of “\(x\) is rational”: “\(x\) is irrational (for real \(x\))”.

Methods of proof

  • Direct: assume \(p\), deduce \(q\).
  • Contrapositive: assume \({\sim}q\), deduce \({\sim}p\) (proves \(p\to q\)).
  • Contradiction: assume \({\sim}\)(statement) and derive a contradiction (e.g. \(\sqrt2\) is irrational).
  • Cases / exhaustion, counter-example (to disprove), construction (to prove existence).
  • Mathematical induction (next card).
  • Pigeonhole principle: if \(n+1\) objects are placed in \(n\) boxes, some box holds at least two; \(kn+1\) objects in \(n\) boxes give a box with at least \(k+1\).

Principle of mathematical induction

To prove \(P(n)\) for all integers \(n\ge n_0\):

  1. Base: verify \(P(n_0)\).
  2. Step: assume \(P(k)\) is true for some \(k\ge n_0\) and prove \(P(k+1)\).

Then \(P(n)\) holds for all \(n\ge n_0\). Strong induction: assume \(P(n_0),\ldots,P(k)\) all true and prove \(P(k+1)\).

Results routinely proved by induction:

\[\sum_{r=1}^nr=\frac{n(n+1)}2,\ \ \sum r^2=\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}6,\ \ \sum r^3=\left[\frac{n(n+1)}2\right]^2,\ \ \sum_{r=1}^n\frac1{r(r+1)}=\frac n{n+1}\]
  • \(2^n\gt n\); \(2^n\gt n^2\) for \(n\ge5\); \(n!\gt2^{n-1}\) for \(n\ge3\); \((1+x)^n\ge1+nx\) for \(x\ge-1\) (Bernoulli).
  • \(n^3-n\) is divisible by 6; \(n(n+1)(n+2)\) by 6; \(7^n-3^n\) by 4; \(a^n-b^n\) by \(a-b\); \(11^{n+2}+12^{2n+1}\) by 133; \(2^{3n}-1\) by 7.
  • De Moivre’s theorem and the binomial theorem for positive integral \(n\); \(\dfrac{d}{dx}x^n=nx^{n-1}\) for \(n\in\mathbb N\).

Switching circuits and logic gates

Circuit / gateLogic
Two switches in series\(p\wedge q\) (AND): current flows iff both are closed
Two switches in parallel\(p\vee q\) (OR): current flows if either is closed
NOT\({\sim}p\): the complementary switch (closed when \(p\) is open)
NAND / NOR\({\sim}(p\wedge q)\) / \({\sim}(p\vee q)\); each alone can build every other gate (universal gates)
XOR / XNOR\(p\oplus q\) / \(p\leftrightarrow q\)

Simplify circuits with the laws of logic; two circuits are equivalent iff their statements have the same truth table.

33
Statistics & logic

Sets and Relation

Laws of the algebra of sets

  • Commutative: \(A\cup B=B\cup A;\ A\cap B=B\cap A\)
  • Associative: \((A\cup B)\cup C=A\cup(B\cup C);\ (A\cap B)\cap C=A\cap(B\cap C)\)
  • Distributive: \(A\cup(B\cap C)=(A\cup B)\cap(A\cup C);\ A\cap(B\cup C)=(A\cap B)\cup(A\cap C)\)
  • De Morgan: \((A\cup B)'=A'\cap B';\ (A\cap B)'=A'\cup B'\)
  • Identity: \(A\cap U=A;\ A\cup\phi=A\)
  • Complement: \(A\cup A'=U,\ A\cap A'=\phi,\ (A')'=A\)
  • Idempotent: \(A\cap A=A,\ A\cup A=A\)

Number of elements in sets

If \(A,B,C\) are finite sets and \(U\) is the finite universal set:

\[n(A\cup B)=n(A)+n(B)-n(A\cap B)\]\[n(A-B)=n(A)-n(A\cap B)\]\[n(A\cup B\cup C)=n(A)+n(B)+n(C)-n(A\cap B)-n(B\cap C)-n(A\cap C)+n(A\cap B\cap C)\]

Exactly two of \(A,B,C\):

\[n(A\cap B)+n(B\cap C)+n(C\cap A)-3n(A\cap B\cap C)\]

Exactly one of \(A,B,C\):

\[n(A)+n(B)+n(C)-2n(A\cap B)-2n(B\cap C)-2n(A\cap C)+3n(A\cap B\cap C)\]

Types of relations on a set \(A\)

  • Void relation: \(\phi\subseteq A\times A\), so it is a relation on \(A\), called the void or empty relation.
  • Universal relation: \(A\times A\subseteq A\times A\), so it is a relation on \(A\), called the universal relation.
  • Identity relation: \(I_A=\{(a,a):a\in A\}\). Every element is related to itself only.
  • Reflexive relation: \(R\) is reflexive if every element of \(A\) is related to itself, i.e. \((a,a)\in R\) for all \(a\in A\). It is not reflexive if some \(a\in A\) has \((a,a)\notin R\).
  • Symmetric relation: \((a,b)\in R\Rightarrow(b,a)\in R\) for all \(a,b\in A\), i.e. \(aRb\Rightarrow bRa\).
  • Transitive relation: \((a,b)\in R\) and \((b,c)\in R\Rightarrow(a,c)\in R\) for all \(a,b,c\in A\), i.e. \(aRb\) and \(bRc\Rightarrow aRc\).
  • Equivalence relation: \(R\) is reflexive, symmetric and transitive.

Every identity relation is reflexive, but every reflexive relation is not an identity relation.

Basic definitions and number sets

  • Set notations: roster \(\{1,2,3\}\), set-builder \(\{x:x\in\mathbb N,\ x\lt4\}\). \(\in\), \(\notin\), \(\subseteq\) (subset), \(\subset\) (proper subset), \(\varnothing\) (empty set), \(U\) (universal set).
  • \(\mathbb N=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}\), \(\mathbb W=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}\), \(\mathbb Z\), \(\mathbb Q\), \(\mathbb R\), \(\mathbb C\); \(\mathbb N\subset\mathbb W\subset\mathbb Z\subset\mathbb Q\subset\mathbb R\subset\mathbb C\). Irrationals \(=\mathbb R-\mathbb Q\).
  • Intervals: \((a,b)\), \([a,b]\), \((a,b]\), \([a,b)\); \(\mathbb R=(-\infty,\infty)\).
  • Types: finite/infinite, singleton, empty, equal sets (\(A\subseteq B\) and \(B\subseteq A\)), equivalent sets (same cardinality), disjoint sets (\(A\cap B=\varnothing\)).
  • \(\varnothing\subseteq A\) for every \(A\); \(A\subseteq A\); \(A\subseteq B\) and \(B\subseteq C\Rightarrow A\subseteq C\).
  • \(A\subseteq B\iff A\cap B=A\iff A\cup B=B\iff B'\subseteq A'\iff A-B=\varnothing\).

Power set, subsets and counting

  • A set with \(n\) elements has \(2^n\) subsets, \(2^n-1\) proper subsets, \(2^n-1\) non-empty subsets, \(2^n-2\) non-empty proper subsets; subsets with exactly \(r\) elements: \(^nC_r\).
  • Power set \(P(A)\) has \(2^n\) elements. If \(n(A)=m\), \(n(B)=n\): \(n(A\times B)=mn\); number of relations from \(A\) to \(B\) is \(2^{mn}\); number of functions \(n^m\).
  • \(P(A)\cap P(B)=P(A\cap B)\); \(P(A)\cup P(B)\subseteq P(A\cup B)\).
  • Ordered pairs \((A,B)\) of subsets of an \(n\)-set: \(4^n\) in all; with \(A\subseteq B\): \(3^n\); with \(A\cap B=\varnothing\): \(3^n\); with \(A\cup B=U\): \(3^n\); with exactly one common element: \(n\cdot3^{n-1}\). (Assign each element to a region; each element has that many choices.)

Operations and their identities

  • Union \(A\cup B\); intersection \(A\cap B\); difference \(A-B=A\cap B'\); complement \(A'=U-A\).
  • Symmetric difference \(A\triangle B=(A-B)\cup(B-A)=(A\cup B)-(A\cap B)\); \(n(A\triangle B)=n(A)+n(B)-2n(A\cap B)\); commutative, associative; \(A\triangle A=\varnothing\); \(A\triangle\varnothing=A\); \(A\cap(B\triangle C)=(A\cap B)\triangle(A\cap C)\).
  • Absorption: \(A\cup(A\cap B)=A\); \(A\cap(A\cup B)=A\). \(A-(B\cup C)=(A-B)\cap(A-C)\); \(A-(B\cap C)=(A-B)\cup(A-C)\).
  • \(A\cup B=A\cap B\Rightarrow A=B\). \(A\cap B=A\cap C\) and \(A\cup B=A\cup C\Rightarrow B=C\).
  • \(n(A\cup B)\le n(A)+n(B)\); \(n(A\cap B)\le\min\{n(A),n(B)\}\); \(n(A')=n(U)-n(A)\).
  • \(A\times(B\cup C)=(A\times B)\cup(A\times C)\); \(A\times(B\cap C)=(A\times B)\cap(A\times C)\); \(A\times B=\varnothing\iff A=\varnothing\) or \(B=\varnothing\).

Venn-diagram counting: more cases

  • At least two of \(A,B,C\): \(n(A\cap B)+n(B\cap C)+n(C\cap A)-2n(A\cap B\cap C)\).
  • None of \(A,B,C\): \(n(U)-n(A\cup B\cup C)\). Only \(A\): \(n(A)-n(A\cap B)-n(A\cap C)+n(A\cap B\cap C)\).
  • General inclusion–exclusion: \(\left|\bigcup_{i=1}^nA_i\right|=\sum|A_i|-\sum|A_i\cap A_j|+\sum|A_i\cap A_j\cap A_k|-\cdots+(-1)^{n+1}|A_1\cap\cdots\cap A_n|\).
  • Surveys: set up the Venn diagram from the innermost region (triple intersection) outwards.

Relations: basic notions

  • A relation \(R\) from \(A\) to \(B\) is a subset of \(A\times B\). Domain \(=\{a:(a,b)\in R\}\), range \(=\{b:(a,b)\in R\}\), codomain \(=B\). For \(n(A)=m\), \(n(B)=n\): total relations \(2^{mn}\).
  • Inverse relation \(R^{-1}=\{(b,a):(a,b)\in R\}\); domain of \(R^{-1}\) = range of \(R\). Composite \(S\circ R=\{(a,c):\exists b,\ (a,b)\in R,\ (b,c)\in S\}\).
  • Antisymmetric: \((a,b)\in R\) and \((b,a)\in R\Rightarrow a=b\). Partial order: reflexive, antisymmetric, transitive (e.g. \(\le\) on \(\mathbb R\), “divides” on \(\mathbb N\), \(\subseteq\) on sets).
  • Equivalence relation: reflexive, symmetric, transitive; examples: equality; “is parallel to”; “is similar to”; congruence modulo \(n\); “has the same remainder”; “same age”.
  • \(R\) is symmetric iff \(R=R^{-1}\); \(R\) is transitive iff \(R\circ R\subseteq R\); \(R\) is reflexive iff \(I_A\subseteq R\).
  • \(R^{-1}\) of an equivalence relation is an equivalence relation. The intersection of two equivalence relations on \(A\) is an equivalence relation; the union need not be.
  • Symmetric and transitive together do not imply reflexive: e.g. \(R=\varnothing\) on a non-empty set, or \(R=\{(1,1)\}\) on \(\{1,2\}\). (They do imply it if every element is related to something.)

Equivalence classes and partitions

  • Equivalence class of \(a\): \([a]=\{x\in A:(a,x)\in R\}\). Then \(a\in[a]\); \([a]=[b]\iff(a,b)\in R\); two classes are either equal or disjoint; the union of all classes is \(A\).
  • The distinct classes form a partition of \(A\); conversely every partition defines an equivalence relation. Congruence modulo \(n\) on \(\mathbb Z\) has \(n\) classes \([0],[1],\ldots,[n-1]\).
Number of relations on a set of \(n\) elementsCount
All relations\(2^{n^2}\)
Reflexive\(2^{n^2-n}\)
Symmetric\(2^{n(n+1)/2}\)
Reflexive and symmetric\(2^{n(n-1)/2}\)
Antisymmetric\(2^n\cdot3^{n(n-1)/2}\)
Equivalence relations (Bell number \(B_n\))\(1,2,5,15,52,\ldots\) for \(n=1,2,3,4,5\)

Checking the properties of a relation

  • Reflexive: show \(aRa\) for every \(a\); disprove with one \(a\) having \((a,a)\notin R\).
  • Symmetric: show \(aRb\Rightarrow bRa\); a single pair with \((a,b)\in R\) and \((b,a)\notin R\) disproves it.
  • Transitive: \(aRb,\ bRc\Rightarrow aRc\); look for \((a,b),(b,c)\in R\) with \((a,c)\notin R\).
  • Common checks: \(R=\{(a,b):|a-b|\le1\}\) is reflexive and symmetric, not transitive; \(R=\{(a,b):a\lt b\}\) is transitive but neither reflexive nor symmetric; \(R=\{(a,b):a\mid b\}\) on \(\mathbb N\) is reflexive, antisymmetric and transitive (a partial order), not symmetric.
End of booklet
